My dear Mr. Secretary:
January
In view of the present restrictions imposed on the
representatives of our American motion picture companies
894.4O61 MOTION PICTURES/32
in Japan who distribute our pictures there and with the
announcement of the enforcement of a new motion picture
law there January 1, 1940, the American motion picture
industry as represented by this Association feels that
the time has arrived to request the kind offices of your
Department to file an official protest through the United
States Ambassador in Japan with the Japanese Government
against such restrictions as placed on our industry.
The representatives of American motion picture com-
panies in Japan under a group calling themselves The
American Motion Picture Association of Japan have talked
this matter over personally with the American Ambassador
following which conversation they filed a report with the
necessary data pertaining to the trouble which dates back
to the time of Japan's invasion of China.
The attached documents we feel explains the matter
very fully and relate to the agreements with the supposed
representatives of the Japanese Government which have not
been fulfilled on their part.
If appropriate instructions could be forwarded to the
American Ambassador in Japan regarding the protection of
the American motion picture interests there it would be
FILID
very greatly appreciated by the whole of the motion picture
industry.

With very best wishes, I am,
Most sincerely,

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
16th November 1939.
H. E. the Hon. Joseph C. Grew,
American Ambassador to Japan,
American Embassy,
Tokyo.
Your Excellency:
The American Motion Picture Association, expressing their
sincere appreciation for the interview granted on the 13th inst.,
beg to submit below a resume of our present problems concerning
the importation of American pictures into Japan and the remittance
of funds to our parent companies in New York. In dealing with our
problems we believe it would be advisable to segregate them under
three headings.
1.
PRESENT AGREEMENT BETWEEN THE FINANCE MINISTRY
AND THE AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSOCIATION.
Since the outbreak of the so-called China Incident, the Ameri-
can Motion Picture Companies in formulating plans to continue busi-
ness during the emergency period without embarrassing the financial
and economic policies of the Government, came to an agreement with
the Finance Ministry. The original approach in connection with this
agreement was made by a certain Mr. Hisaji Kubo, a personal friend
of the then Finance Minister, Mr. O. Kaya. Mr. Kubo having obtained
letters of introduction from the Foreign Ministry in Japan, and from
the late Ambassador to the United States, Mr. Saito, presented him-
self as an Official Representative of the Japanese Authorities to the
foreign managers of the American Distributing Companies in New York,
with certain proposals designed to enable the Japanese Government
and the American Motion Picture Distributors to come to mutually
acceptable terms for the conduct of future operations in Japan. The
entire matter was referred by our Head Offices to the American Motion
Picture Association in Japan. Several conferences took place between
Mr. H. Kubo and the American Motion Picture Assn., in Tokyo, as a
result of which this Association agreed to pay Mr. Kubo Y30,O00 as a
gratuity if, and when, the agreement with the Finance Ministry would
be completed. At the same time the American Motion Picture Assn.
held several conferences with various members of the Finance Ministry.
After protracted negotiations an Agreement was finally made with the
Finance Ministry and under date of August 10, 1938, the American
Motion Picture Assn. received an Official Document from the Finance
Ministry signed by Mr. Kojiro Nakamura, Head of the Exchange Bureau.
This document outlines in detail the entire arrangement, under which
the members of the American Motion Picture Assn. representing the
eight major Motion Picture Distributing Companies of the United States
would be permitted to import a limited number of pictures amounting
to $30,0o0 print cost, figured at the rate of 1: cents per foot, and
to remit to their respective Head Offices in New York accumulated fun
amounting to the sum of Y3,000,000 under a deferred payment plan of
three years without interest.
The entire arrangement should have been completed by December
3lst 1938 as stipulated in the agreement.

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The agreement was worded in such a way as to give the Finance
Ministry an opportunity to deviate from the terms of same. However,
despite considerable delay, the Finance Ministry have issued permits
covering the remittances of the entire amount of Y3,000,00 as specifie
in the agreement and also permits for the importation of pictures up t
the amount of U. S. $20,O00. The last import permits were issued by
the Finance Ministry only in September this year, or exactly nine
months after the expiration date of the agreement.
On the strength of the agreement the members of the American
Motion Picture Association have ordered a considerable supply of pic-
tures which have subsequently arrived at Yokohama and have been kept
there in readiness for immediate importation on receipt of permits.
The members have also obligated themselves to considerable expense in
preparing for the resumption of their normal business activities in
Japan.
On repeated advice from the Finance Ministry the American Motion
Picture Association paid Mr. Aisaji Kubo, who presented himself as
the promoter of the entire plan to the Ministry, the sum of Y30,000
as a gratuity for his efforts in bringing about the consummation of
the entire arrangement.
Since the restrictions on imports of pictures and export of
capital were put into effect by the Japansese Government, a great
number of Japanese Films have been, and are still being exported to
the United States. Home Ministry reports give the following statistic
covering export censor permits issued for Japansese films for shipment
to the U.S. (Sept. 1937 has been taken as the time at which the ban
on imports of American films into Japan was enforced):
Sept. - Dec. 1937 84 Feature Films

These figures show that Japan exports more film to Americar.
than anywhere else, and also that Japanese footage imported into
America is greater than that from any other single country. Besides
all these are being admitted into the U.S. without any difficulty

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
whatever, neither is there any restriction as regards payment for
them. As compared with the above export figures, the eight American
companies of this Association have, since the ban was enforced and
up to date, received permits for approximately $20,000 print cost
covering 1,330,000feet of imports.
The American Motion Picture Association exhausted every possible
avenue of approach to speed up the fulfillment of the said agreement
At present no indication is evident as to the time of issuance of
import permits covering the balance of U. S. $10,O00 print cost.
While we fully realize that the actual legality of the document
received from the Finance Ministry is debatable, the fact that the
Finance Ministry have carried out the agreement to its present extent
and also that there was an implied acknowledgement of the agreement in
their request to pay Mr. Kubo the gratuity mentioned above, would seem
to indicate that the Authorities accepted the agreement in good faith:
Furthermore, the American Motion Picture Assn. have been repeatedly
told by the Finance Ministry that as long as this agreement is not
completed, no other method of importation or remittances could be ex-
pected.
The members of this Association earnestly solicit Your
Excellency's assistance in presenting their case to the proper
authorities. We might mention that the Foreign Office is fully
cognisont of all these negotiations and in fact have on a number of
occasions expressed their interest in it and desire to see the matter
satisfactorily completed.
2.
THE NEW MOTION PICTURE LAW.
At the end of September the Home inistry officially notified the
American Motion Picture Association of the provisions of the new
Motion Picture Law. The Law will be enforced from January 1st 1940.
Under a provision of this aw, the members of the American Motion Pic
ture Association will be placed on a quota basis for the distribution
of pictures in Japan. All indications are that the quota for each
individual distributor will be extremely small as compared to the
average number of pictures imported and distributed by the members of
this Association heretofore.
The Home Ministry, however, in discussing the application of the
New Motion Picture Law with various members of the Industry have indi
cated that they intend to take into consideration the diplomatic and
economic relationship existing between Japan and various other
countries. Of the total number of foreign pictures distributed in
Japan in normal times, the American pictures occupied the major
portion. Even since the restrictions were put into effect, the
American Motion Picture Distributors represented by this Association,
have been importing and releasing approximately 80% of the total
foreign product.
As the Home Ministry are still undecided as to the quota
allocation for each individual distributor, this Association believes

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
that it might be timely to make a representation through diplomatic
channels to impress upon the Authorities the difference in the stand-.
ing of the American Distributors as compared to the independent dis-
tributors dealing in pictures from other countries. The members of
this Association have already made their representation to the Home
Ministry, however, no tangible results were obtained. Simultaneously
with our approach to Your Excellency, the American Motion Picture Assn.
is making a similar report to the Hays Organization with a request to
bring our case to the attention of the State Department and to make a
suitable petition to embody the Motion Picture Industry into any
possible negotiations between the United States and Japan, so that we
may be permitted to continue business on a reasonable scale.
3.
FROZEN FUNDS.
Since February 1939 - the last remittance under our present
agreement with the Finance Ministry - no remittances whatsoever have
been allowed. We estimate that at present funds accumulated in Japan
by the members of this Association amount to Y4,O00,000. Assuming that
the present agreement is entirely completed, the attitude of the
Finance Ministry leads us to believe that the possibility of further
remittances is remote. The continued accumulation of funds will be-
come an intolerable situation for our Head Offices.
We are, therefore, particularly anxious to arrive at some
arrangement with the Finance Ministry for future remittances and we
trust that this question may be included in the representations that
we petition Your Excellency to make. We have also included this pro-
blem in our letter to the Hays Organization, with a request to embody
same into their representation to the State Department.
We are, Your Excellency,
Yours very truly,
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSOCIATION OF JAPAN
(sd) R. D. Spierman
Hon. Secretary.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
May 26th, 1939
O. Kaya, Esq.,
Tokyo.
Sir:
We wish to take this opporturity of reiterating our
gratitude for your kind efforts on our behalf with the
Minister of Finance, which resulted in the conclusion of an
agreement between the Finance Ministry of the Imperial Japanese
Government and the American Motion Picture Association of Japan.
This, as you will undoubtedly recall, was the outcome of the
acceptance by the Director of the Exchange Control Bureau of
the proposals offered by the said Association.
According to this agreement, the Finance Ministry undertook to
permit the eight American motion picture distributors in Tokyo,
who comprise the American Motion Picture Association of Japan,
the importation of motion picture prints to the extent of
$30,O00, and to remit to their respective Head Offices in New
York accumulated funds aggregating ¥3,000,000 in total under a
deferred payment plan of three years without interest. The
entire arrangement should have been completed by December 3lst
1938 as stipuleted in the agreement.
The provisions regarding the remittances of funds have been
completed. Unfortunately, however, this is not the case with
regard to the undertakings covering the importation of pictures.
Between the time the agreement was signed on August 10th 1938
and this date, only $15,000 worth of pictures, or one-half of
the amount, has been permitted us for import:tion.
In spite of the repeated visits to the Exchange Control Bureau of the
Finance Ministry, we have until now been unable to obtain any
indication as to the possible dates on which the permits for the
balance of the pictures will be granted. This Association was
informed by the Exchange Control Bureau that they would be in a
position to grant us at least a portion of the balance of the
permits after the new Motion Picture Law had been passed by the
Diet.
Since then, representatives of this Association have called many
times at the Exchange Control Bureau of the Finance Ministry
with a view to finding out the Dates on which we may expect permits
for the balance, but were advised that no definite promises could
be made as to the dates on which permits for the balance of the
pictures will be granted.

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Our parent companies in New York and our Hollywood executives
have accepted the entire agreement in good faith and expected the
same to be carried out in accordance with its provisions and on
the dates specified. We, the American Motion Picture Association
of Japan, have been requested by our parent companies to give them
reasons and explanations for the continual delay, and to approach
the Japanese Government with a view to clarifying its intentions
regarding the fulfillment of this agreement, so that they, our New
York Head Offices, and Hollywood producers may decide on certain
policies of action.
The American Motion Picture Association of Japan has already
addressed letters to "Variety" and other leading motion picture
trade papers in America with a view to forestalling any reports in
connection with the above mentioned agreement that might create an
undesirable impression or be interpreted as a reflection on the
word of the Japanese Government. These letters have been published
with very favorable comment. A copy is attached hereto. We are
particularly desirous of maintaining such a position that we will
be able to continue to exert our utmost efforts towards checking
any anti-Japanese tendencies in American motion picture circles,
which so far have been negligible.
The members of the American Motion Picture Association of Japan
represent the eight biggest motion picture companies in the united
States which have been accleimed as the leading factors in the
motion picture industry throughout the world. The American
motion picture industry represents a very powerful force of
public opinion in the United States as weli as throughout the
world, having at its command tremendous resources, and producing
pictures not only for entertainment but also for cultural purposes
as well as for the purpose of dissemination of news through the
medium of newsreel pictures.
As the present position taken by the Ministry of Finance
indicates unwiliingness on the part of the Japanese Government
to carry out the agreement in its original form, this Association
fears that this attitude may be interpreted by our respective
Head Offices as a desire of the Japanese Government to abrogate
the agreement. This matter may not be of great importance from
the Japanese Government's viewpoint, but Japan's claim to fair
play and justice would seem to infer the strict fulfillment of any
agreement undertaken. In view of this, the stand now being taken
by the Exchange Control Bureau of the Finance Ministry cannot be
regarded as anything but unfair. Such an attitude on the part of
the Japanese Government may result in creating a bad impression
on the American motion picture producers and the American public
as a whole, which will naturally affect the bonds of friendship
existing between the two countries.

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Because of the delay and the seriousness of the situation
as it affects our business, several of our Head Offices
have dispatched their special representatives to Japan to
investigate this matter. These representatives have
expressed considerable dissatisfaction at the slow
progress of the execution of the entire arrangement.
Several members of this Association have been requested
by their respective Head Offices to return to the
United States for the purpose of reporting and explaining
the true status of the motion picture industry in Japan
in general, the reasons for the dlay in the granting of
the permits for the balance of the pictures as stipulated
in the aforesaid agreement, to make reports relative to
new legislation affecting our industry and finally to
present the opinions and submit the recommendations of
this Association as to the future prospects for American
motion picture activity in Japan.
The American Motion Picture Association in Japan has done
everything in its power up to the present time to prevent
the dissemination of unfavorable propaganda through the
medium of motion pictures, always desiring and anxious to
maintain the traditional friendly relations existing between
Japan and the United States. It is because of this desire
that we earnestly beg you tolend us your good offices and
influence towards the speedy fulfillment of the above
mentioned agreement by the Ministry of Finance.
Respectfully yours,
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSIN OF JAPAN
Chairman

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
10th August, 1939.
S. Ikeda, Esq.,
Tokyo.
Sir:
Approximately two months ago, the AMPA addressed a petition to
Your Excellency. We believe that it received your kind attention,
for shortly thereafter, the Finance Ministry issued us import
permits to the amount of $2,05l. However, we are sure you will
realize that, lacking any indication of subsequent permits, this
quantity fillis but an extremely small part of the requirements of
the market for the immediate future. Under the circumstances, we
feel constrained to approach Your Excellency again for further
consideration of our agreement with the Finance Ministry signed
in August 1938.
Despite the above-mentioned agreement, whereby film imports to
the extent of $30,o00 were to be permitted by the end of last
year, we have so far received permits only amounting to approximately
$17,500. In the last seven months, permits have been issued to us
only once -- this was in June and, as stated above, amounted to
only $2,051.
Realising the situation and the wishes of the government, this
Association earlier in the year proposed to the Finance Ministry
a postponement of several months beyond the deadline specified in
the agreement, and requested an approximate schedule of imports
over the extended period. While the Ministry of Finance was at
that time favorably inclined towards some such amicable arrangement,
actually nothirg was effected and the matter simply allowed to
lapse indefinitely.
As it is already over seven months since the agreement should have
been fulfilled and just a year since the agreement was signed, our
Head Offices are making constant inquiries of us, and are showing
unmistakable signs of impatience and concern over the status of
our agreement with the Japanese Government. This Association is
placed in the extremely embarrassing position of trying to overcome
any erroneous or unfavorable impressions while at the same time

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
being unable to offer any explanations as to the continued
delay.
Another point of prime importance is the question of substitute
permits for films which are banned by the authorities. Because
of the recent tightening of censorship requirements, several
films have been rejected. While we are perfectly willing to
abide by any such rulings which the government may think to be
to the best advantage of the nation, on the other hand, frankly,
we fail to understand the Finarce Ministry's objection to allowing
a substitute picture for each one as rejected. Although assurances
in this regard were given us verbally in early discussions, we are
at present encountering great difficulty with our applications for
such replacements.
We wish also to take this opportunity of mentioning that import
permits have not always been issued proportionately to the
individual quotas of the separate companies. In other words, there
is considerable divergence among the companies in the proportion
of permits granted to total allotment. The highest company has
received 62% of their import quota, while the lowest is 53%. We
are very anxious to have all companies treated as equitably as
possible in this matter of allocation.
A great number of Japanese films are being exported to the United
States. Home Ministry reports give the following statistics
covering export censor permits issued for Japanese films for
shipment to the U.S. (Sept.1937 has been taken as the time at
which the ban on imports of American films into Japan was enforced):
Sept. - Dec. 1977
84 Feature films
631,700 feet
Short subjects & news
203.800
Total .....
835,500 ft.
Jan. - Dec. 1938
210 Feature films
1,328,800 feet
Shorts & news
525,500
Total.
1,854,300 ft.
Jan. - June 1939
110 Feature films
651,100 feet
Shorts & news
319,300
Total.
970,400 ft.
Grand Total since import ban
3,660,200 ft.
(404 Feature films plus shorts & news)

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
These figures show that Japan exports more film to America
than anywhere else, and also that Japanese footage imported
into America is greater than that from any other single
country. Besides, all these are being admitted into the U. S.
without any difficulty whatever, neither is there any restric-
tion as regards payment for them.
As compared with the above export figures, the eight American
companies of this Association have, in the same period since
the ban was enforced, received permits for approximately $17,500
covering 1,835,000 feet of imports. During this year, we have
received only one set of permits -- this was in June and amounted
to only $2,051, or 136,700 feet.
Notwithstanding the extremely severe conditions with which
American films are burdened in Japan, there have as yet been no
countervailing measures or reprisals in America. Although we
have received suggestions (which are perfectly natural) to
appeal to the American Embassy, we have adopted the stand that
this is a purely business transaction between the Finance Ministry
and ourselves, and we have conseguently followed the policy of
confining it to these limits. We feel that this is the more
desirable method for both parties, as we see no reason why the
entire agreement cannot be fulfilled in an amicable and mutually
satisfactory manner without involving diplomatic representations.
In the case of the agreement in question, as the remaining
balence of $12,500 is not a sum of any great magnitude we most
earnestly request that, in the interests of goodwill and with
sincerity of purpose, these import permits be issued, and that
we, the American companies operating here, be accorded treatment
more in line with that given to Japunese films in America.
Respectfully yours,
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASS N OF JAPAN
Chairnan

aittance Division, Confidential No. 1l.
August 10, 1938
Mr. Michael M. Bergher,
Representative of American Motion Picture
Association of Japan.
We hereby inform you that it is expected that the matters re-
lating to the importation of American motion pictures under credit,
which have been applied for under date of May 28, 1938, through Mr.
Hisaji Kubo will be approved in accordance with the following terms
and conditions:
1.
To be allowed to import pictures on or before the last day of
1938, up to the maximum of $30,000 in print costs (this amount
may be changed by mutual arrangement according to changes in
the foreign trade of this country in the future); the minimum
price for one foot shall be l cents.
2.
In connection with the time and quantity of importation of said
pictures, an importation plan for each month shall be filed on
or before the last day of the preceding month and the approval
of the competent authorities shall be obtained therefor.
3.
In respect of the importation of said pictures, each company
shall be required to obtain permission under Article 1 or
Article 4 - (2) of Department of Finance Order No. 1 of 1937
at each time of importation.
4.
Importation of pictures of such kind as are not suitable to
the present extraordinary situation of this country will not
be allowed.
5.
The royalty obtained from the exhibition of the imported pic-
tures may be remitted to America through the Yokohama Specie
Bank up to Y3,000,000 on or before the last day of 1938 (in-
clusive of unremitted royalty of approximate Y2,000,000 accumu-
lated during the period from September, 1937 to May 15, 1938)
6.
In respect to the time and number of installments of re-
mittances of said royalties and the amount of each installment,
a remittance plan shall previously be filed and the approval
of the competent authorities shall be obtained therefor.
7.
In respect to the remittance of said royalties, each company
shall be required to obtain permission under Article 3 of
Department of Finance Order No. 7 of 1933 at each time of re-
mittance.
8.
The amount remitted with the aforesaid permission shall be
converted into American currency and shall be deposited with
the San Francisco Branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank without
interest and shall not be paid to the payee before full three
years have elapsed after the completion of a remittance.
9.
If the amount of royalty received does not reach Y3,O00,O00 at
the end of this year, permission for remittance shall be limited
in the amount thus received.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
10.
In regard to royalties received, each company shall immediately
report to the competent authorities the amount as on may 13 and
July 31, 1938, and shall also report the amount on the last day
of each month including August, l938, and for months thereafter
on or before the loth of the following month. Provided that
as to bank deposits a certification of such bank shall be
attached.
11.
If any of the applicant companies should violate the laws and
regulations of the foreign exchange control or any of the fore-
going paragraphs or otherwise act impropertly, an approval in
question shali be cancelled.
12.
The matters relating to the reshipment of imported pictures, the
amount borne by the principal office and the supply of raw films
shall have nothing to do with an approval in question.
Kojiro Nakamura
Head of Exchange Bureau
of the Department of
Finance (Office Seal)

(Document annexed to communication)
(from Department of Finance
Outline of Memorandum produced bym
American Motion Picture Association of
Japan (consisting of eight companies)
1。
Plan for remittance and importation of films.
(a) We wish to obtain permission for the remittance of
Y3,O00,000 (including unremitted royalties of approxi-
mately Y2,000,000 which were accumulated from September
of last year to this date) before the end of this year.
provided that said amount will be paid to Yokohema
Specie Bank which Bank will convert the amounts into
American dollars and transmit them to its New York
Branch, and they will be paid over to the addressees
by the New York Branch of the Yokabama Specie Bank
after three years. No interest shall accrue.
(b) We wish to obtain permission for the importation of pic-
tures in the maximum of $30,000 in print costs before
the end of this year. For this $30,0o0 we wish to ob-
tain permission for immediate remittance or payment by
draft on each arrival of pictures.
2.
The application for permission for the above shall be made
in the joint names of the eight companies and the Department
of Finance shall give one single permit complying with the
foregoing conditions.
3.
The allotment of Y3,000,000 and $30,000 shal1 be made by the
relative eight companies and the remittance and importation
shall be made by each company in accordance with the per-
mission granted in the joint names and within the limits
granted thereby.
4.
The invoice price (print cost) of an ordinary picture shall
be ls cents per foot. As to colored pictures, it is necessary
to import several duplicates in addition to the original print,
because no duplicate of colored pictures can be made in Japan.
The print cost for one print to be regarded as the original
print will be included in the $30,000, but the print costs of
duplicates shall be not considered in this figure and each time
separate applications for permission for importation of dupli-
cates will be made and we wish to import such duplicates if
permitted.
Further we request the following:
1.
We wish to obtain permission for the re-shipment of pictures
which were once landed at Yokohama but were not imported and
imported original prints from which duplicates were made in
Japan.
2.
We wish to obtain permission for remittances of amount borne
by principal offices for which permission was granted until
February of this year but no permission granted thereafter.
3.
We wish to have ample supplies of raw film so that we will be
able to make duplicates in Japan.

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.99)
FINANCE DEPARTMENT
QRDINANCE NO. L. 1937
Article 1.
Notwithstanding the provisions of Article 4, Nos. 1 and 11 of
Binance Department Ordinance No. 7, 1933, no transactions or acts
specified in Article 3 of the sai d Ordinance (including the carry-
ing out of future contracts for foreign exchange entered into priob-
to the enforcement of this Ordinance ) may be performed for effect-
ing settlement necessary to the importation of goods into Japan or
localities in which Japanese currency is legal tender except with
the permission of the Mintster of Finance, except in the following
cases:-
L. For performing transactions or acts the total value of
which for one month does not exceed an equivalent of
Y30,000; (Amended to read Y1,000,00 by Ordinance No. 23
on July 7, 1937). Subsequently revised to Y100.00 in
December 1937.
2.
For selling, or paying, or buying exchange in order to
pay, exchange under letters of credit acquired with
permission in accordance with the provisions of Article
2 or acquired without permission in accordance with the
proviso of the said Article;
3.
When necessary for settling documentary bills for goods
the importation or shipment from abroad of which has been
completed when this Ordinance came into force.
4.
For carrying out future contracts for foreign exchange
entered into prior to the enforcement of this Ordinance
in order to settle documentary bills for goods shipped
from abroad within one week after the enforcement of this
Ordinance.

=== Page 16 === (confidence 1.00)
Article 4-(2) of Finance Department
Ordinance No. 1 of 1937
Article 4-(2) except with permission of the Minister of
Finance no person may import goods into this country without a draft
for their value in whole or in part, nor import goods into this
country without a draft in yen for their value in whole or in part,
which draft is addressed to this country, from a territory where
Japanese currency is effective, except in the following cases;
(1) Importation of goods relating to transactions or acts
necessary for the settlement of the price under Articles
1 - 4, for which permission was obtained.
(2) Importation of goods relating to transactions or acts
necessary for the settlement of the price under the pro-
visos of Articles 1 or 2, for which no permission is re-
quired.
(3) Importation of goods as samples or gifts or for the pur-
pose of exhibiting at an exposition.
(4) Importation of returned goods which were exported from
this country.
(5) Importation of goods for repairing.
(6) Importation of fish caught and loaded on a vessel which
sailed from this country.
(7) Importation of personal articles or house-removing
articles.
(8) Importation of goods necessary for governmental authorities.
(9) Except the foregoing cases, to import goods the value of
which do not exceed Yl,Ooo per month.

=== Page 17 === (confidence 1.00)
ARTICLE 3 of Department of Finance
Order of 1933
Article 3.
Except with the permission of the Minister of Finance the follow-
ing transactions or acts may not be performed;-
1. The purchase, with Japanese currency as a consideration,
of foreign currency, foreign exchange or Yen exchange
drawn on localities where Japanese currency is legal
tender;
2.
The sale of foreign exchange, with Japanese currency as
a consideration, to parties other than foreign exchange
banks or with the object of setting off the same against
exchange bought;
The sale and purchase, with foreign currency as a con-
sideration, of Yen exchange which is foreign exchange;
4.
The transaction or taking of (Japanese) currency, foreign
currency, cheques, bills, or notes or remittances to
foreign countries by any method not provided for by
Article 1 and other Numbers of this Article;
5.
Payments in localities where this Ordinance is in force
under instructions given abroad.

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. S. Ikeda
October 1st 1938.
Your Excellency:
We beg to respectfully submit to your kind attention the follow-
ing brief resume of the status of the American Motion Picture Industry
in Japan.
Last year when the Government decided to prohibit the importation
of foreign motion pictures because of the China Incident, the American
Motion Picture Association of Japan, in formulating plans to continue
business during the emergency period without embarrassing in any way
the financial and economic situation of the Government, came to an
agreement with various members of the Finance Ministry after protracted
discussions. By this agreement, the members of the American Motion Pic
ture Association, representing the eight major companies of the United
States, were permitted to import a limited number of pictures,
aggregating in total to the sum of Thirty Thousand Dollars ($30,o00)
print cost and to remit to their respective head offices in New York
the royalties earned on these pictures and funds accumulated up to the
time permits were granted for remittances under this agreement, a total
sum amounting to Three Million Yen (Y3,O00,O00) under a deferred pay-
ment plan of three years without interest.
The respective head offices in New York of the members of the
American Motion Picture Association of Japan accepted this plan after
which the agreement was definitely made with the Finance Ministry and
under date of August lOth, 1938, the American Motion Picture Assoc-
iation received an official document from the Finance Ministry, signed
by Mr. Kojiro Nakamura, head of the Exchange Bureau. This agreement
outlined in detail the entire arrangement.
Upon receipt of this agreement, the American Motion Picture
Association of Japan telegraphed to the respective head offices in New
York that the agreement was duly and officially consummated and would
go into effect within a few weeks after that date as we were officially
advised.
On the strength of this advice of the Finance Ministry, all
members of the American Motion Picture Association of Japan ordered a
considerable supply of pictures which have subsequently arrived at
Yokohama and the members have also obligated themselves to considerable
expense in preparation for the resumption of their business activities
in Japan.
On September 26th, 1938, permits were granted by the Finance
Ministry to remit the first installment of the Three Million Yen
(Y3,000,O00) amounting to Yen Seven Hundred and Fifty Thousand (Y750,00d
in total for all members of the Association which remittances have al-
ready been made. However, no permits for the importation of pictures
have been received by the American Motion Picture Association, with the
exception of a negligible number of cartoons.
On September 30th, the committee of the Association was officially
notified by the head of the Remittance Department and head of Section 1

=== Page 19 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
of the Import Department that because of pressure brought by a
group of small independent Japanese distributing companies which re-
quest that no importation of pictures should be permitted to the
members of the American Motion Picture Association until after some
arrangement had also been consummated with that group and that it
would be necessary to hold up indefinitely the permits for the im-
portation of pictures for the members of the American Motion Picture
Association until such time as the matter would be settled to the
satisfaction of the above-mentioned Japanese distributors. The
American Motion Picture Association of Japan believes that it is un-
fair and unjust to regard them in the same light as the group of
Japanese importers mentioned above.  The majority of the Japanese
distributors of foreign pictures in Japan who comprise the above-
mentioned group, are newly organized companies, mostly during the
period of emergency and are importing only a negligible quantity of
very inferior product produced by insignificant American and European
producers.
On the other hand, the members of the American Motion Picture
Association of Japan represent the eight biggest motion picture com-
panies in the United States and have been acclaimed as the leading
factors of Motion Picture Industry throughout the world. The members
of the American Motion Picture Association represent a very powerful
force of public opinion in the United States as well as throughout
the world, having at their command tremendous resources and are pro-
ducing pictures not only for amusement but for cultural purposes, as
well as for purposes of dissemination of newsreel pictures.
As the notice given to the committee of the Association by the
Department heads of the Remittance and Import Sections of the Finance
Ministry clearly indicates that te entire agreement between the
American Motion Picture association of Japan and the Finance Ministry
may be nullified, the American Motion Picture Association therefore
appeals to Your Excellency, in the name of justice and fair-play of
our cause which is bound up with the spirit of the promotion of
friendly relations between Japan and the United States. The American
Motion Picture Association feels that it will be a matter of great
regret if the agreement once approved by the Government may be abro-
gated. This may result in misunderstood conception on the part of
the American people, of the true desires of the Japanese Government
and may affect the bonds of friendship now existing.
The American Motion Picture Association desires to bring to Your
Excellency's attention the fact that immediately upon the conclusion
of the agreement, two members of the Association have left for the
United States who were instructed by the Association to recommend and
prevail upon the respective executives of the members of the Assoc-
iation in New York to discontinue the very unfavorable propaganda
which is being disseminated through the activities of newsreels in
the United States.
The Association therefore hopes that Your Excellency will realize
the seriousness of the issue involved, and give this matter your kind
and personal attention.
Most respectfully yours,
American Motion Picture Association of
Chairman
Japan.

THE REGULATIONS CONCERNING
THE ENFORCEMENT OF THE
MOTION PICTURE LAW.
Article 1.
Persons who intend to obtain a permit for motion picture
production business under the provisions of Article 2,
Para. 1 of the Motion Picture Law shall file the Applica-
tion for a permit in triplicate, stating therein the
particulars mentioned hereunder to the Home Minister and
the Education Minister through the office of the
prefectural governor competent over the territory where
the principal office of the motion picture production
business concerned exists (in the prefecture of Tokyo,
the Director of the Metropolitan Police Board, same
context following):-
1. Full name and domicile;
2. Scope of business;
3. The place where the motion picture studio exists;
4. The structure, installations and equipment of
the motion pict re studio:-
A. Entire plan of the construction (to attach
the drawings);
B. Constriction of the building, installations
and equipments employed for photographing,
sound recording, developing, orinting, projecting
and storing (to attach the drawings);
C. The kinds and number of cameras, recording
and printing machines;
D. Electric installations (to attach drawings).
5. The kinds of motion pict tres to be produced;
6. Quantity of motion picture prints to be produced in a ye
7. Number of employees and their respective kinds of work;
8. The time when the business is to be commenced.
To the Application for a permit of the preceding paragraph
shall the prospectus of industry and the tentative state-
ment of profit and loss account be attached.
When the party intends to alter the particulars of No.2,
No.3, No.4, A and/or B, No.5 or No.8 of Para. 1 hereof,
he shall obtain the permission of the Home Minister and
Education Minister, through the office of the prefectural
governor, submitting the reasons for alteration in accor-
dance with the example of Para.l hereof.
When the particulars of No.l of Para. 1 hereof have been
altered, the notification in duplicate shall be without
delay sent in to the Home Minister and Education Minister
through the office of the prefectural governor according
to the provisions of Para. 1 hereof.
Article 2.
Persons who intend to obtain a permit for the motion
picture distribution as provided fer in Article 2, Para.1
of the Motion Picture Law, shall submit an application in

Article 26.
The motion picture which was censored in accordance with
the provisions of Article 13, Para. 1 of the Motion
Picture Law shall be dealt with as disqualified if it falls
within the purview of any one of the following numbers:-
1. Pictures that are calculated to dehase the dignity of the
Imperial Household or to spoil the prestige of the Empire;
2. Pictures that are calculated to be prejudicial to the
interest of the Empire from either political, military,
diplomatic or economic view points or otherwise;
3. Pictures that are calculated to give rise to misunder-
standing as to the national culture;
4. Pictures the art, technique, or technicalities of
production of which are particularly inferior;

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
5. Pictures that are otherwise unsuitable for export.
rticle 27.
In case where motion pictures that have been censored in
accordance with the provisions of Article 14, Para. 1 of
the Motion Picture Law fall within the purview of any of
the following numbers, they shall fail to be qualified:-
1. Pictures that are calculated to debase the dignity of the
Imperial Household or to spoil the prestige of the Empire;
2. Pictures that are calculated to propagade the ideology
in defiance of the Imperial Constitution;
3. Pictures that are calculated to be impedimental from
the political or military, diplomatic or economical points
of view and/or the view point of public interest.
4. Pictures that are calculated to defy the good manners
or to spoil the national moral;
5. Pictures that are calculated to be especially detrimental
to the purity of the national language;
6. Pictures the art, technique or technicalities of produc-
tion of which is particularly inferior;
7. Pictures that are calculatec to otherwise retard the
progress of the national culture.

stating therein the particulars mentioned in No.1 to No.4
of Article 25, Para.1 together with the print of motion
picture calling for the sanction and two copies of play-book
(one of the two copies containing the Seal-of-Passege of the
Form No.8).
Article 37.
When the sanction has been rendered in accordance with the
provisions of Article 35, Para.1, the Seal-of-Sanction
of the Form No.9 shall be affixed on the play-book of the
very motion picture print on which the Seal-of-Passage of
the Form No.8 is being affixed.
Article 38. The term of validity of the sanction of Article 35, Para.1
shall be one and the same as the term of validity granted for
the motion picture itself upon passage of the censorship.
Article 39.
The Home Minister may, in cases of special circumstances,
cancel the sanction granted under Article 35, Para. 1.
When the sanction has been cancelled in accordance with
the provisions of the preceding paragraph, the Seal-of-
Sanction affixed on the playbook of the very motion picture
shall be struck out by erasure.
Article 40.
When the Seal-of-Passage has been re-affixed on the play-
book in accordance with the provisions of Article 32,
Para.1 with respect to the motion picture which receivea
the sanction of Article 35, Para. 1, the playbook
concerned shall be without delay produced before the
Education Minister applying for re-affixation of the
Seal-of-Sanction of the form No.9.

=== Page 30 === (confidence 1.00)
-11-
41. When the prefectural governor intends to cause the
motion picture to be exhibited in accordance with the
provisions of Article 15, Para. 2 of the Motion Picture
Law, the period of such exhibition shall be fixed as
within one week at a time, provided, however, that such
exhibition shall not be of a period put together in excess
of six weeks per year for one cinematographic theatre.
Article 42. The motion picture exhibitor who operates a permanent cinema-
tographic theatre shall not exhibit dramatic foreign motion
pictures in excess of fifty prints as aggregated for the
whole year and in respect of a certain cinematographic theatre.
In case where the exhibitor of the preceding paragraph ex-
hibits a dramatic foreign motion picture that has been played
at the same theatre of his, such picture shall not be calcula-
ted into the number of prints of the preceding paragraph.
In the event an exhibitor commences the exhibition of
dramatic foreign motion pictures afresh in the middle of
the year, the number of prints proviced for in Para. 1
hereof shall be computed by the number of months.
Article 43. The motion picture exhibitor who operates a permanent
cinematographic theatre shall not have the exhibition
lasting beyond three hours at a time, provided, however,
that, in case where the exhibition is effected in accord-
ance with the provisions of Article 15, Para. 2 of the
Motion Picture Law, extension shall be allowed within a
length of time not exceeding thirty minutes.
Article 44. The motion picture exhibitor or any other person who
exhibits a motion picture otherwise than for business shall
not have the prints projected on the screen at a speed of
over 27.4 metres a minute.
Article. 45. The motion picture exhibitor or the one who exhibits the
motion picture otherwise than for business shall not be
allowed to exhibit unless with the projector equipped with
the automatic safety-switches.
Article 46. The motion picture exhibitor or one who exhibits the motion
pictures otherwise than for business, shall not allow
minors under fourteen of age to be admitted into the place
of exhibition with the exception of the case where the
pictures played are exclusively those passed by censorship
in accordance with the provisions of Article 14, Para. 1
and recognized by the Education Minister (in case of a
picture passed the censorship of the prefectural governor,
the governor concerned) to be harmless fromminor's educa-
tional point of view, provided, however, that this rule
shall not prevail where the minors under six of age are in
company with a guardian.
Article 47.
When the recognition of the preceding Article has been
granted, the Seal-of-Recognition after the Form No.10
shall be affixed on the playbook containing the Seal-of-
Passage ofthe Form No.8, provided, however, that in the

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
32-
case of the prefectural governor's recognition such seal
may well be omitted.
The provisions of Article 38 to Article 40 shall apply to
the recognition of the preceding Article with necessary
modifications.
Article 48.
The motion picture exhibitor or the one who exhibits the
motion picture otherwise than for business shall not allow
to operate the projection machine by other than those with
projection license granted by the prefectural governor,
provided, however, that this rue shall not prevail where
the film concerned is non-inflammable. The motion picture
exhibitor or one who exhibits the motion picture other-
wise than for business shall install two sets of projec-
tion machines, and, in case the exhibition lasts as a rule
six hours consecutively, three, and in case of less hours,
two, projection licensees shall be put to work.
Article 49.
In case the full name and domicile shall be entered in
the Application or in the Notification according to the
provisions of this Ordinance, the trade-name, the address
of the principal office, the full name and domicile of the
representative thereof shall be entered in the case of the
party concerned being a juridical person.
Article 50.
Persons who contravened the rules of Article 3; 4; 11,
Para. 1 and of Article 33, shall be subject to a minor fine.
Article 51.
In case his agents, head of his family, member of his family,
inmste, employee or wbsoever works under him shall have com-
mitted an act falling within the purview of the provisions
of the preceding Article, the motion picture producer,distri-
butor or one owning the motion picture that has passed the
censorship shall not be exempt from punishment on the excuse
that this particuar act had not been in accordance with his
direction.
Article 52.
The criminal provisions of Article 50 shall be applied to
the member of the board, the director or any other officer
who executes the affairs in case the offender is a
juridical person, and to the legal agent in case the
offender is a minor or a person incompetent of managing
the property, provided, however, that this rle shall not
prevail where the minor concerned is legally competent
same as an adult in respect of the business concerned.
ADDITIONAL PROVISIONS
Article 53.
This Ordinance shall be enforced as from the day on which
the Motion Picture Law takes effect, provided, however,
that the provisions of Article 20, Article 42 and Article
46 shall take effect as from January 1st 1940, those of
Article 35, Para. 2 as from January 1st 1940 so far as tne
cities of Tokyo, Kyoto, Osaka, Yokohama, Kobe and Nagoya
are concerned and as from July 1st 1940 so far as any
other cities, towns or villages are concerned, and that the
provisions of Article 45 and 48 shall take effect as from
Öctober 1st 1940.

Article 56.
The term of validity of the censored films that have been
censored by the administrative authorities prior to enforce-
ment of this ordinance or the film which passed the
inspection for export shall be the remeinder of the term
of validity as governed by the old rules.
Article 57.
The motion picture exhibitors shall not be allowed to
exhibit motion pictures, withn the interval of six months
as from the time of the provisions of Article 35, Para. 2
taking effect, with the exception of the number of days
equivalent to fifty percent of the playdays of each month,
as according to the rules of the said paragraph.
In cases where the motion picture exhibitors exhibit films
that have passed the censorship of the administrative
authorities prior to this Ordinance having taken effect
or such duplicates thereof as having received the sanction
of Article 35, Para. 1, the provisions concerning meterage
as mentioned in said Article Para. 2 shall not prevail
during the intervel of three years as from the time when this
Ordinance has taken effect.
Article 58.
In cases where censored films that have passed the censor-

nance coming into effect are either exclusively exibited
or else along with the motion pictures that have received
sanction under the rule of Article 46 hereof, with the
exception of the case where the Education Minister regards
it specificaily necessary, the rules of Article 46 shall
not prevail.

16th November 1939.
. . the Hon. Joseph C. Grew,
Americam Ambassador to Japan,
American Embassy,
Tokyo.
Your Excellency:
The American Motion Picture Association, expressing their
sincere appreciation for the interview granted on the l3th inst.,
beg to submit below a resume of our present problems concerning
the importation of American pictures nto Japan and the remittance
of funds te our parent companies in New York. In dealing with our
problems we believe it would be advisable to segregate them under
three headings.
1.
PRESENT AGREEMENT BETWEEN THE FINANCE MINISTRY
AND THE AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSOCIATION.
Bince the outbreak of the so-called China Incident, the Ameri-
can Motion Picture Companies in formulating plans to continue busi-
ness during the emergency period without embarrassing the financial
and economic policies of the Government, came to an agreement with
the Finance Ministry. The original approach in connection with this
agreement was made by a certain Mr. Hisaji Kubo, a personal friend
of the then Finance Minister, Mr. O. Kaya. Mr. Kubo having obtained
letters of introduction from the Foreign Ministry in Japan, and from
the late Ambassador to the United States, Mr. Saito, presented him-
self as an Official Representative of the Japanese Authorities to the
foreign mamagers of the American Distributing Companies in New York,
with certain proposals designed to enable the Japanese Government
and the American Motion Picture Distributors to come to mutually
acceptable terms for the conduet of future operations in Japan. The
entire matter was referred by our Head Offices to the American Motion
Pieture Association in Japan. Several conferences took place between
Mr. H. Kubo and the American Motion Picture Assn., in Tokyo, as a
result of which this Association agreed to pay Mr. Kubo Y30,000 as a
gratuity if, and when, the agreement with the Finance Ministry would
be completed. At the same time the American Motion Picture Assn.
held several conferences with various members of the Finance Ministry.
After protracted negotiations an Agreement was finally made with the
Finance Ministry and under date of August 10, 1938, the Ameriean
Motien Pioture Assn. received an Official Document from the Finance
Ministry signed by Mr. Kojiro Nakamura, Head of the Exchange Bureau.
This document outlines in detail the entire arrangement, under which
the members of the American Motion Picture Assn. representing the
eight major Motion Picture Distributing Companies of the United Stater
weuld be permitted to impert a limited number of pictures amounting
to $s0,000 print cost, figured at the rate of lt cents per feet, and
to remit te their respective Head Offices in New York accumulated fum
amounting to the sum of 18,000,000 under a deferred payment plan of
three years without interest.
The emtire arrangement should hmve been completed by December
Slst 19s8 as stipulated in the agreement.

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The agreement was worded in such a way as to give the Finance
Ministry an opportunity to deviate from the terms of same. However,
despite considerable delay, the Finance Ministry have issued permits
cevering the remittances of the entire amount of Y3,000,00 as specified
in the agreement and also permits for the importation of pictures up to
the amount of U. S. $20,000. The last import permits were issued by
the Finance Ministry only in September this year, or exactly nine
months after the expiration date of the agreement.
On the strength of the agreement the members of the American
Motion Pieture Association have ordered a considerable supply of pic-
tures which have subsequently arrived at Yokohama and have been kept
there in readiness for immediate importation on receipt of permits.
The members have also obligated themselves to considerable expense in
preparing for the resumption of their normal business activities in
Japan.
On repeated advice from the Finance Ministry the American Motion
Picture Association paid Mr. Sisaji Kubo, who presented himself as
the promoter of the entire plan to the Ministry, the sum of Y30,000
as a gratuity for his efforts in bringing about the consummation of
the entire arrangement.
Since the restrictions on imports of pictures and export of
capital were put into effect by the Japansese Government, a great

the United States. Home Ministry reports give the following statistios

pected.
The members of this Association earnestly solicit Your
Excellency's assistance in presenting their case to the proper
authorities. We might mention that the Foreign Office is fully
cognisont of all these negotiations and in fact have on a number of
occasions expressed their interest in it and desire to see the matter
satisfactorily completed.
2.
THE NEW MOTION PICTURE LAW.
At the end of September the Home inistry officially notified thi
American Motion Picture Association of the provisions of the new
Motion Picture Law. The Law will be enforced from January 1st 1940.
Under a provision of this Law, the members of the American Motion Pie
ture Association will be placed on a quota basis for the distribution
of piotures in Japan. All indications are that the quota for each
individual distributor will be extremely small as compared to the
average number of pictures imported and distributed by the members of
this Association heretofore.
The Home Ministry, however, in discussing the application of the
New Motion Pioture Law with various members of the Industry have indi
cated that they intend to take into consideration the diplomatic and
economic relationship existing between Japan and various other
countries. Of the total number of foreign pictures distributed in
Japan in normal times, the American pictures occupied the major
portion.Even since the restrictions were put into effect, the
Ameriean Motion Picture Distributors represented by this Association
have been importing and releasing approximately 80% of the total
foreign product.
As the Home Ministry are still undecided as to the quota
allocation for each individual distributor, this Association believe

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
that it might be timely to make a representation through diplomatio
channels to impress upon the Authorities the difference in the stand-
ing of the American Distributors as compared to the independent dis-
tributors dealing in piotures from other countries. The members of
this Association have already made their representation to the Home
Ministry, however, no tangible results were obtained. Simultaneously
with our approach to Your Excellency, the American Motion Picture Assn.
is making a similar report to the Hays Organization with a request to
bring our case to the attention of the State Department and to make a
suitable petition to embody the Motion Picture Industry into any
possible negotiations between the United States and Japan, so that we
may be permitted to continue business on a reasonable scale.
3.
FROZEN FUNDS.
Since February 1939 - the last remittance under our present
agreement with the Finance Ministry - no remittances whatsoever have
been allowed. We estimate that at present funds accumulated in Japan
by the members of this Association amount to Y4,000,000. Assuming that
the present agreement is entirely completed, the attitude of the
Finance Ministry leads us to believe that the possibility of further
remittances is remote. The continued accumulation of funds will be-
come an intolerable situation for our Head Offices.
We are, therefore, particularly anxious to arrive at some
arrangement with the Finance Ministry for future remittances and we
trust that this question may be included in the representations that
we petition Your Excellency to make. We have also included this pro-
blem in our letter to the Hays Organization, with a request to embody
same into their representation to the State Department.
We are, Your Excellency,
Yours very truly,
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSOCIATION OF JAPAN
(sd) R. D. Spierman
Hon. Secretary.

May 26th, 1939
O. Kaya, Esq.,
Tokye.
sir:
We wish to take this opportunity of reiterating our
gratitude for your kind efforts on our behalf with the
Minister of Finance, which resulted in the conclusion of an
agreement between the Finance Ministry of the Imperial Japanese
Government and the American Motion Picture Association of Japan.
This, as you will undoubtedly recall, was the outcome of the
asceptance by the Director of the Exchange Control Bureau of
the proposals offered by the said Association.
Aecording to this agreement, the Finance Ministry undertook to
permit the eight American motion picture distributors in Tokyo,
who comprise the American Motion Picture Association of Japan,
the importation of motion picture prints to the extent of
$30,000, and to remit to their respective Head Offices in Mew
York accumulated funds aggregating 3,000,000 in total under a
deferred payment plan of three years without interest. The
entire arrangement should have been completed by December 3lst
1958 as stipulated in the agreement.
The provisions regarding the remittances of funds have been
completed. Unfortunately, however, this is not the case with
regard to the undertakings covering the importation of pictures.
Between the time the agreement was signed on August 10th 1938
and th1s date, only $15,000 worth of pictures, or one-half of
the amount, has been permitted us for importation.
In spite of the repeated visits to the Exchange Control Bureau of the
Finanee Ministry, we have until now been unable to obtain amy
indication as to the possible dates on which the permits for the
balance of the pictures will be granted. This Association was
informed by the Exchange Control Bureau that they would be in a
position to grant us at least a portion of the balance of the
permits after the new Motion Picture Law had been passed by the
biet.
Simce then, representatives of this Association have called many
times at the Exchange Control Buresu of the Finance Mimistry
with a view to finding out the Dates on which we may expect permits
for the balanee, but were advised that no definite promises could
be made as to the dates on which permits for the balance of the
pietures will be granted.

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
一 2 -
Our parent companies in New York and our Hollywood executives
have ascepted the entire agreement in good faith and expected the
same to be earried out in accordance with its provisions and on
the dates specified. We, the American Motion Picture Association
of Japan, have been requested by our parent companies to give them
reasons and explanations for the continual delay, and to approach
the Japanese Government with a view to clarifying its intentions
regarding the fulfillment of this agreement, so that they, our New
York Head Offices, and Hollywood producers may decide on certain
pelicies of action.
The American Motion Picture Association of Japan has already
addressed letters to "Variety" and other leading motion picture
trade papers in America with a view to forestalling any reports in
eomneotion with the above mentioned agreement that might create an
umdesirable impression or be interpreted as a reflection on the
word of the Japanese Government. These letters have been published
with very favorable comment. A copy is attached hereto. We are
particularly desirous of maintaining such a position that we will
be able to continue to exert our utmost efforts towards checking
any anti-Japanese tendencies in American motion picture circles,
which so far have been negligible.
The members of the American Motion Picture Association of Japan
represent the eight biggest motion picture companies in the United
States which have been acclaimed as the leading factors in the
motion picture industry throughout the world. The American
motion picture industry represents a very powerful force of
public opinion in the United States as weli as throughout the
world, having at its command tremendous resources, and producing
pictures not only for entertainment but also for cultural purposes
as well as for the purpose of dissemination of news through the
medium of newsreel pictures.
As the present position taken by the Ministry of Finance
indicates unwiliingness on the part of the Japanese Government
to carry out the agreement in its original form, this Association
fears that this attitude may be interpreted by our respective
Head Offiees as a desire of the Japanese Government to abrogate
the agreement. This matter may not be of great importance from
the Japanese Govermment's viewpoint, but Japan's claim to fair
play and justice would seem to infer the strict fulfillment of any
agreement undertaken. In view of this, the stand now being taken
by the Exehange Control Bureau of the Finance Ministry cannot be
regarded as anything but unfair. Such an attitude on the part of
the Japamese Govermment may result in creating a bad impression
on the American motion picture producers and the American public
as a whole, which will naturally affect the bonds of friendship
existing between the two countries.

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
一8-
Beeause of the delay and the seriousness of the situation
as it affects our business, several of our Head Offices
have dispatched their special representatives to Japan to
investigate this matter. These representatives have
expressed considerable dissatisfaction at the slow
progress of the execution of the entire arrangement.
Several members of this Association have been requested
by their respective Head Offices to return to the
United States for the purpose of reporting and explaining
the true status of the motion picture industry in Japan
in general, the reasons for the lay in the granting of
the permits for the balance of the pictures as stipulated
in the aforesaid agreement, to make reports relative to
now legislation affecting our industry and finally to
present the opinions and submit the recommendations of
this Association as to the future prospects for American
motion picture activity in Japan.
The American Motion Picture Association in Japan has done
everything in its power up to the present time to prevent
the dissemination of unfavorable propaganda through the
medium of motion pictures, always desiring and anxious to
maintain the traditional friendly relations existing between
Japan and the United States. It is because of this desire
that we earnestly beg you to bnd us your good offices and
influence towards the speedy fulfillment of the above
mentioned agreement by the Ministry of Finance.
Respectfully yours,
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE ASSIN OF JAPAN

Approximately two months ago, the AmPA addressed a petition to
Your Exeellency. We believe that it received your kind attention,
for shortly thereafter, the Finance Ministry issued us import
permits to the amount of $2,05l. However, we are sure you will
realize that, lacking any indication of subsequent permits, this
quantity filis but an extremely small part of the requirements of
the market for the immediate future. Under the circumstances, we
feel constrained to approach Your Excellency again for further
consideration of our agreement with the Finance Ministry signed
in August 1988.
Despite the above-mentioned agreement, whereby film imports to
the extent of $s0,000 were to be permitted by the end of last
year, we have so far received permits only amounting to approximately
$17,s00. In the last seven months, permits have been 1ssued to us
only once -- this was in June and, as stated above, amounted to
only $2,051.
Realising the situation and the wishes of the government, this
Association earlier in the year proposed to the Finance Ministry
a postponement of several months beyond the deadline specified in
the agreement, and requested an approximate schedule of imports
over the extended period. While the Ministry of Finance was at
that time favorably inelined towards some such amicable arrangement,
actually nothing was effected and the matter simply allowed to
lapse indefinitely.
As it is already over seven months since the agreement should have
been fulfilled and just a year since the agreement was signed, our
Head Offices are making constant inquiries of us, and are showing
ummistakable signs of impatience and concern over the status of
our agreement with the Japanese Govermment. This Associatiom is
placed in the extremely embarrassing position of trying to overcome
any erroneous or unfavorable impressions while at the same time

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
being unable to offer any explanations as to the continued
delay.
Another point of prime importance is the question of substitute
permits for films which are banned by the authorities. Because
of the recent tightening of censorship requirements, several
films have been rejected. While we are perfectly willing to
abide by any such rulings which the government may think to be
to the best advantage of the nation, on the other hand, frankly,
we fail to understand the Finance Ministry's objection to allowing
a substitute picture for each one as rejected. Although assurances
in this regard were given us verbally in early discussions, we are
at present encountering great difficulty with our applications for
such replacements.
We wish also to take this opportunity of mentioning that import
permits have not always been issued proportionately to the
individual quotas of the separate companies. In other words, there
is considerable divergence among the companies in the proportion
of permits granted to total allotment. The highest company has
received 62% of their import quota, while the lowest is 53%. We
are very anxious to have all companies treated as equitably as
possible in this matter of allocation.

and ourselves, and we have consequently followed the poliey of

desirable method for both parties, as we see no reason why the
entire agreement cannot be fulfilled in an amicable and mutually
satisfactory manner without involving diplomatic representations.

balance of 12, 500 is not a sum of any greet magnitude we most

sineerity of purpose, these import permits be issued, and that
we, the American companies operating here, be accorded treatment
more in line with that given to Japanese films in America.

2.
In connection with the time and quantity of importation of said
pictures, an importation plan for each month shall be filed on
or before the last day of the preceding month and the approval
of the competent authorities shall be obtained therefor.
8.
In respect of the importation of said pictures, each company
shall be required to obtain permission under Article 1 or
Article 4 - (2) of Department of Finance Order No. 1 of 1937
at each time of importation.
4.
Importation of pictures of such kind as are not suitable to
the present extraordinary situation of this country will not
be allowed.
5.
The royalty obtained from the exhibition of the imported pic-
tures may be remitted to America through the Yokohama Specie
Bank up to Y3, 000,000 on or before the last day of 1938 (1n-
clusive of unremitted royalty of approximate Y2,000,000 accumu-
lated during the period from eptember, 1937 to May 15, 1938)
8.
In respect to the time and number of installments of re-
mittances of said royalties and the amount of each installment,
a remittance plan shall previously be filed and the approval
of the competent authorities shall be obtained therefor.
7.
In respect to the remittance of said royalties, each company
shall be required to obtain permission under Article 3 of
Department of Finance Order No. 7 of 1933 at each time of re-
mittance.
8.
The mount remitted with the aforesaid permission shall be
converted into American currency and shall be deposited with
the San Francisco Branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank without
interest and shall not be paid to the payee before full three
years have elapsed after the completion of a remittance.
9.
If the amount of royalty received does not reach Y3,000,000 at
the end of this year, permission for remittance shall be limited
in the amount thus received.

-2
10. In regard to royalties received, each company shall immediately
report to the competent authorities the amount as on May 13 and
July 31, 1988, and shall also report the amount on the last day
of each month including August, 1938, and for months thereafter
on or before the loth of the following month. Provided that
 to bank deposits a certification of such bank shall be
attached.
1l. If any of the applicant companies should violate the laws and
regulations of the foreign exchange control or any of the fore-
going paragraphs or otherwise act impropertly, an approval in
question shall be cancelled.
12.
The matters relating to the reshipment of imported pictures, the
amount borne by the principal office and the supply of raw films
shall have nothing to do with an approval in question.

Outline of Memorandum produced bym
American Motion Picture Association of
Japan (consisting of eight companies)
1. Plan for remittance and importation of films.
(a) We wish to obtain permission for the remittance of
Y3,000,000 (including unremitted royalties of approxi-
mately Y2,000,000 which were accumulated from September
of last year to this date) before the end of this year.
provided that said amount will be paid to Yokohema
Specie Bank which Bank will convert the amounts into
American dollars and transmit them to its New York
Branch, and they will be paid over to the addressees
by the New York Branch of the Yokbaama Specie Bank
after three years. No interest shall accrue.
(b) We wish to obtain permission for the importation of pic-
tures in the maximum of $30,000 in print costs before
the end of this year. For this $30,000 we wish to ob-
tain permission for immediate remittance or payment by
draft on each arrival of pictures.
8.
The application for permission for the above shall be made
in the joint names of the eight companies and the Department
of Finance shall give one single permit complying with the
foregoing conditions.
8.
The allotment of Y3,000,000 and $30,000 shall be made by the
relative eight companies and the remittance and importation
shall be made by each company in accordance with the per-
mission granted in the joint names and within the limits
granted thereby.
The invoice price (print cost) of an ordinary picture shall
be lt cents per foot. As to colored pictures, it is necessary
to import several duplicates in addition to the original print,
because no duplicate of colored pictures can be made in Japan.
The print cost for one print to be regarded as the original
print will be included in the $30,000, but the print costs of
duplicates shall be not considered in this figure and each time
separate applications for permission for importation of dupli-
cates will be made and we wish to import such duplicates if
permitted.
Further we request the following:
1.
We wish to obtain permission for the re-shipment of pictures
whieh were once landed at Yokohama but were not imported and
imperted original prints from which duplicates were made in
Japan.
2.
We wish to obtain permission for remittanoes of amount borne
by prinoipal offices for which permission was granted until
February of this year but no permission granted thereafter.
3.
We wish to have ample supplies of raw film so that we will be
able to make duplieates in Japan.
