=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. 8. Ikda
October 1st 1938.
Your Excellency:
We beg to respectfully submit to your kind attention the follow-
ing brief resume of the status of the American Motion Picture Industry
in Japan.
Last year when the Government decided to prohibit the importation
of foreign motion pictures because of the China Incident, the American
Motion Picture Association of Japan, in formulating plans to continue
business during the emergency period without embarrassing in any way
the financial and economic situation of the Government, came to an
agreement with various members of the Finance Ministry after protracted
discussions. By this agreement, the members of the American Motion Pic-
ture Association, representing the eight major companies of the United
States, were permitted to import a limited number of pictures,
aggregating in total to the sum of Thirty Thousand Dollars ($30,000)
print cost and to remit to their respective head offices in New York
the royalties earned on these pictures and funds accumulated up to the
time permits were granted for remittances under this agreement, a total
sum amounting to Three Million Yen (Y3,000,000) under a deferred pay-
ment plan of three yeurs without interest.
The respeotive head offices in New York of the members of the
American Motion Picture Association of Japan accepted this plan after
which the agreement was definitely made with the Finance Ministry and
under date of August 10th, 1938, the American Motion Picture Assoo-
iation received an official document from the Finance Ministry, signed
by Mr. Kojiro Nakamura, head of the Exchange Bureau. This agreement
outlined in detail the entire arrangement.
Upon receipt of this agreement, the American Motion Picture
Association of Japan telegraphed to the respective head offices in New
York that the agreement was duly and officially consummated and would
go into effect within a few weeks after that date as we were officially
advised.
On the strength of this advice of the Finance Ministry, all
members of the American Motion Picture Association of Japan ordered a
considerable supply of pictures which have subsequently arrived at
Yokohama and the members have also obligated themselves to considerable
expense in preparation for the resumption of their business activities
in Japan.
On September 26th, 1938, permits were granted by the Finance
Ministry to remit the first installment of the Three Million Yen
(Y8,000,000) amounting to Yen Beven Hundred and Fifty Thousand (y750,000
in tetal for all members of the Association which remittances have al-
ready been made. However, no permits for the importation or pictures
have been received by the American Motion Picture Association, with the
exception of a negligible number of cartoons.
On Beptember 30th, the committee of the Association was officially
notified by the head of the Remittance Department and head of Section 1

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of the Import Department that because of pressure brought by a
group of mall independent Japanese distributing companies which re-
quest that no importation of piotures should be permitted to the
members of the American Motion Picture Association until after some
arrangement had also been consummated with that group and that it
would be necessary to hold up indefinitely the permits for the im-
portation of piotures for the members of the American Motion Picture
Association until such time as the matter wouldtbe settled to the
satisfaction of the above-mentioned Japanese distributors. The
American Motion Picture Association of Japan believes that it is un-
fair and unjust to regard them in the same light as the group of
Japanese importers mentioned above. The majority of the Japanese
distributors of foreign pictures in Japan who comprise the above-
mentioned group, are newly organized companies, mostly during the
period of mergency and are importing only a negligible quantity of
very inferior product produced by insignificant American and European
producers.
On the other hand, the members of the American Motion Picture
Association of Japan represent the eight biggest motion picture com-
panies in the United States and have been acclaimed as the leading
factors of Motion Picture Industry throughout the world. The members
of the American Motion Picture Association represent a very powerful
force of public opinion in the United States as well as throughout
the world, having at their coumand tremendous resources and are pro-
ducing piotures not only for amusement but for cultural purposes, as
well as for purposes of dissemination of newsreel pictures.
As the notice given to the committee of the Association by the
Department heads of the Remittance and Import Sections of the Finance
Ministry clearly indicates that te entire agreement between the
Amerioan Motion Picture ssociation of Japan and the Finance Ministry
may be nullified, the American Motion Picture Association therefore
appeals to Your Excellenoy, in the name of justice and fair-play of
our cause which is bound up with the spirit of the promotion of
friendly relations between Japan and the United States. The American
Motion Picture Association feels that it will be a matter of great
regret if the agreement once approved by the Government may be abro-
gated. This may result in misunderstood conception on the part of
the American people, of the true desires of the Japanese overnment
and may affect the bonds of friendship now existing.
The American Motion Picture Association desires to bring to Your
Bxcellenoy's attention the fact that immediately upon the conclusion
of the agreement, two members of the Association have left for the
United States who were instructed by the Association to recommend and
prevail upon the respective executives of the members of the Assoc-
iation in New York to discontinue the very unfavorable propaganda
whieh is being disseminated through the activities of newsreels in
the United States.
The Association therefore hopes that Your Exeellency will realis
the serioumess of the issue involved, and give this matter your kind
and personal attention.
Most respectfully yours,
American Motion Pioture Association of
Chairmam
Japan.

The motion picture which was censored in accordance with
the provisions of Article 13, Para. 1 of the Motion
Pieture Law shall be dealt with as disqualified if it falls
within the purview of any one of the following numbers:-
1. Pictures that are calculated to dehase the digmity of the
Imperial Household or to spoil the prestige of the Empire
2. Pietures that are calculated to be prejudicial to the
interest of the Empire from either politiocal, military,
diplomatic or economic view points or otherwise;
S. Fictures that are calculated to give rise to misunder-
standing as to the national culture;
4. Piotures the art, technique, or techniealities of
production of whioh are particularly inferior;

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)
5. Pictures that are otherwise unsuitable for export.
Artiole 27.
In case where motion pictures that have been censored in
accordance with the provisions of Article 14, Para. 1 of
the Motiom Picture Law fall ithin the purview of any of
the following numbers, they shall fail to be qualifieds-
1. Pictures that are calculated to debase the dignity of the
Imperial Household or to spoil the prestige of the Empire;
2. Piotures that are calculated to propagade the ideology
in defiance of the Imperial Constitution!
3. Pictures that are calculated to be impedimental from
the political or military, diplomatic or economical points
of view and/or the view point of public interest.
4. Piatures that are calculated to defy the good manners
or to spoil the national moral;
5. Pictures that are calculated to be especially detrimental
to the purity of the national language;
6. Pictures the art, technique or technicalities of produo-
tion of which is particularly inferior;
7. Pictures that are calculated to otherwise retard the
progress of the national culture.
Article 28.
As for the pictures which passed the censorship, the Censor-
ship-passage-certificate as per the Form No. 6 shall be
given and the Seal-of-Passage and mark and number be
affixed on the motion picture print with respect to those
covered under the Application of the provisions of Article
24 and the Seal-of-Passage and mark and mumber as per the
Form No.7 shall be affixed on the motion picture print
and Seal-of-Passage as per the Form No.8 on the playbook
with respect to those covered under the Applieation of
the provisions of Article 25, provided, however, that,
as for those which passed the censorship under the
provisions of Article 24, Para. & and of Article 25,
Para. &, the Seal-of-Passage and mark and number to be
otherwise affixed on the motion picture print may well
be omitted.
Artiole 29.
The passage of censorship of Article 13, Para.1 of the
Motion Picture Law shall be valid for a period of three
months, and the passage of censorship of Article 14,
Para. 1 of the same Law for a period of three years,
provided, however, that the passage of censorship
exercised by the prefectural governor in accordance with
the provisions of Article 14, Para. 1 of the same Law
shali be valid for a period of three months and the
validity thereof shall not prevail in other prefecture.
Artiole 80.
In cases where special circumstances exist, the censorship
authorities may, in respect of the motion pictures cen-
sored, designate the term of validity for the passage of
censorship otherwise than that provided for in the pre-
ceding Article or may impose territorial or other
limitation or restriotion.
When the censorship authorities have imposed the limita-
tiom or restrictiom mentioned in the preceding paragraph,
statement to such effect shall be entered im the Censorship-
passage-certificate in respect of ome applied for im

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
accordance with the provisions of Article 24 and in the
play-book in respect of one applied for in accordance with
the provisions of Article 24 and in the play-book in

Artiele 81. When the Home Minister has effected the dispositions
provided for in Article 1s, Para. & of the Motion Picture Law,
the Censorship-passage-certificate shall be caused to be
returned to the authorities and the seal and mark and number
affixed in proof of passage on the print itself be cancelled
in the case of prohibition, and a statement to such effect be
entered in the censorship-passage-certificate in the case of
limitation.
Article 32.
When the motion picture which passed the censorship falls
within the purview of any e of the following mumbers,
re-affixation of the Seal-of-Passage or of mark and
number or re-issuance of the Censorship-passage-certificate
may be applied for to the censorship authorities concerned
of that particular motion picture:-
1. When the Seal-of-Passage or mark and number affixed on
the motion picture print has been destroyed;
2. When the playbook on which the Seal-of-Passage had been
affixed has been lost or destroyed;
8. When the Censorship-passage-certificate has been lost
or destroyed.
In the case of No.& of the preceding Paragraph, a further
copy of playbook shall be produced.
Article 88.
When it is desired that the particulars of No.& or No.7 of
Article 24, Para.1 or of No.2 of Article 25, Para.1 be altered
with respect to the motion picture that has passed the censor-
ship, permission therefor shall be obtained from the censorship
authorities concerned of that particular motion picture.
Article 84.
The censorship authorities shall charge the following fees
with respect to the censorship in accoriance with the provisions
of Article l4, Para.1 of the Motion Picture Law:-
1. As for the motion picture which is subject to censorship
of the Home Minister, one sen per metre or any fraction,
and one sen and five rin per one metre or fraction with

8. As for the duplicate of the motion picture which received

Artiele 35. The motion pictures that shall be caused so be exhibited

References
Reference is made to report from this office No. 34
entitled, "Motion Pictures in Japan", dated October 6,
1938, which dealt with the background and organization
of the motion picture industry in Japan, production as
well as distribution; and report No. 59 entitled, "Japan
Motion Picture Law", dated June 28, 1939, which gave the
text of Motion Picture Law No. 66, approved by the Diet
and promulgated on April 4, 1939, providing for the
stricter control by the Government of the production and
distribution of motion pictures.
Resumé
A new ordinance regulating motion picture importa-
tion, production, and dietribution in Japan was promul-
gated on September 26, 1939, the regulations as to
importation and dietribution affecting the eight American
companies which distribute pictures here. Such companies
are required to file with the Ministry of Home Affairs by
October 3l of each year applications for proposed importa-
tions during the coming year. After approval by the
Ministry of Home Affairs, approval of the Ministry of
Finance must be obtained to cover remittance for the con-
ventional value of 1-1/2 cents per running foot fixed by
the customs as the 1mport valuation. As yet, no approvals
of 1mportations for 1940 have been obtained.
Under the 1938 agreement with the Ministry of Finance,
about l00 American pictures were released in Japan during
1938 and a similar number during 1939. The new ordinance
limits the number of new foreign dramatic motion pictures
exhibited in each theater to 50 per annum besides news
reels

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
reels and cartoons. The new ordinance also limits the
length of programs to three hours. The struggle between
the Toho and Shochiku interests for the control of Japan-
ese picture production and market continues. The raw
film now available in Japan for making prints of motion
pictures is not very satisfactory and on account of the
small number of films which the American companies are
permitted to release, prorits are meager.
Promulgation of Regulations
The American motion picture distributors in Tokyo
awaited with considerable apprehension the promulgation
of the ordinance putting into force the provisions of the
Motion Picture Law covered by report No. 59. It was ant1-
cipated that several restrictions might be imposed, in-
cluding the control by Japanese nationals of a majority
of the capital stock of distributing companies, or even
the setting up of a near-monopoly. However, although the
new ordinance imposed various restrictions, it did not
embody either of the provisione mentioned. It appeared
as Joint Ministerial Ordinance No. 1 of the Ministries of
Home Affairs, Education, and Welfare, having been promul-
gated on September 26, 1939, and published in the Official
Gazette of the following day, Edition No. 3820 of Septem-
ber 27, 1939. An unofficial translation of this ordinance
1/
is attached (Enclosure No. 1) to this report.
Supplementary Ordinances
Moreover, this same edition of the Official Gazette
contained two other ordinances relating to the Motion
Picture Law. The first, Imperial Ordinance No. 667, of
September 26, 1939, fixed the date of October 1, 1939, as
the date upon which most of the provisions of the Motion
Picture

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Pioture Law of April 4, 1939, should go into effect.
The second, Imperial Ordinance No. 668, of the same
date, defined the motion picture manufacturing business
and the motion picture distributing business, and indi-
cated the minister referred to in the Motion Pioture Law
in various paragraphs which imposed certain duties upon
"the competent minister". An unofficial translation of
2/
Ordinance No. 668 1s attached (Enclosure No. 2) to th1s
report.
Scope of Regulations
The new regulations relate to both producing and
distributing companies. However, tne portion relating to
producing companies is of little interest to American
firms and, consequently, this report will be confined to
those features which affect the Amerioan distributing com-
panies here, of which there are eight organized into an
association called the American Motion Picture Association
of Tokyo.
Permits for Distributing Companies
Article 2 of Ordinance No. 1 requires persons or
firms desiring to obtain a permit for motion picture dis-
tribution to file application with the Minister of Home
Affairs and Minister of Education, giving full details
of such persons or companies. This permit is to be filed
through the Prefectural Governor, or in the case of Tokyo,
through the Metropolitan Police, which indeed has hereto-
fore been the government office to deal with the motion
picture companies in Tokyo. Article 5 of the ordinance
requires that before the end of February of each year,
a full account must be rendered to the same authorities
of the past year's business of the company oonoerned
(Interview No. 1).
Allotment

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
lotment of Piotures
Foreign pictures may be imported by any country
during any year only in the quantities allotted by the
Minister of Home Affairs. Article 21 of Ordinance No. 1
provides that each company shall file on or before Octo-
ber 31 of each year an application for the number of
motion pictures whioh it is desired to distribute during
the coming year. The American Motion Picture Association
of Tokyo has heretofore treated as a unit with the Japan-
ese authorities in regerd to the number of pictures to be
imported, obtaining a total of pictures which were allot-
ted by the Association members among themselves. The
American companies desired to continue the practice but
such plan was not approved by the authorities, who now
insist on dealing with each company individually (Inter-
view No. 2).
1938 Agreement
The negotiations carried out in 1938 as to importa-
tion of pictures and remission of frozen funds, and
especially the letter from the Minister of Finance of
August 10, 1938 (see report No. 34 cited ahove) contem-
plated the importation of $30,000 worth of films at 1-1/2
cents per running foot, that is two million running feet
of film. This would be 2o0 films more or less according
to the length of each. All these films were to have been
1mported before the end of l938 but, as a matter of faot,
permission to import only half of this amount was actually
obtained from the Ministry of Finance, the importation
being effected during the last months of 1938 and the
first months of 1939. During 1929 to date, permits were
obtained for the importation of only $5,000 worth of films,
making a total importation under the 1938 agreement of
about

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
$20,000 worth of f11m (Interview No. 2).

1939 has not yet been published but was compiled, upon
request, by the staff of the MOVIE TIMES:

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)

It should be noted that the importation of pictures
into Japan during a given period of time does not corre-
epond with the number released during that period, since
there are always a number of imported pictures in stook
awaiting censorship or a favorable opportunity for release.
American Piotures in Stock
There are at present in stock in Tokyo about sixteen
American pictures divided among the eight American com-
panies. This will be sufficient to supply the theaters
for only eight weeks at the rate of one picture per week
for each of the two principal chains of theaters.
Pictures Requested for 1940
When the various American companies made their appli-
cations on October 31, 1939, for permission to 1mport
pictures during 1940, each of the eight companies applied
for forty pictures, a total of 320 pictures. In addition,
1t is understood that Japanese distributors have applied
for 78 ptctures, making a grand total of 398. Inasmuch
as the American companies have been able to release only
about 100 p1ctures in 1938 and a similar number in 1939,
it is probable that they will be permitted to import alto-
gether not more than l00 pictures during the calendar
year l940, and possibly less, since the total of foreign
piotures, inoluding European, may be limited to loo (Inter-
view No. 2).
Permite from Finance Ministry
It should be borne in mind that the permission of the
Ministry of Home Affairs for the importation of pictures
is not final but that the whole matter has then to be taken
up with the Ministry of Finance in order to obtain the
necessary

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.98)
一 9 -
necessary foreign exohange for the conventional value
of 1-1/2 cents per running foot, which 1s remitted
abroad upon importation of the films. The Ministry of
Finance also muet give its consent for the remittance
abroad of the frozen profits. No remittances of such
funde have been permitted since the 1938 agreement under
which Y3,000,000 was remitted. The frozen profits of
the American distributors have now accumulated to the
amount of about Y4,000,000 (Interview No. 2).
Limitation of Exhibition of Pictures
Article 42 of Ordinance No. 1 provides that each
motion picture house may present during a year only
fifty new foreign dramatic motion pictures, not count-
ing pictures elready exhipited in the same theater.
The latter may be repeated ad libitum. It is understood,
furthermore, that this proviso as to fifty piotures
relates only to feature pictures and not to news reels
or cartoone. This proviso would also appeer to indicate
a total release of about lo0 imported feature pictures
per annum, inasmuch as there are in Tokyo, and the prin-
cipal Japanese cities, two chains of first-run houses,
namely, those controlled by Toho (Toho Motion Picture
Company, Limited) and those controlled by Shochiku
(Shochiku Company, Limited). One hundred pictures would
provide about one picture each week for each of these
two groups. Article 42 takes effect January 1, 1940
(see report No. 34, cited above).
Limitation of Program and Attendance
Article 43 of Ordinance No. 1 provides that motion
pioture programs shall be limited to three hours. How-
ever, this is not a new proviso, having been in force
a8

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
as a police regulation since February 1, 1938. This
limitation of the program has led in some cases to the
severe cutting of pictures in order to reduce their
length (Interview No. 1). Article 46 provides that
minors under fourteen years may attend only programs
espeoially approved for them, this proviso to be
effective January 1, 1940.
German Pictures
It is not presumed that many European pictures will
be brought to Japan during the continuance of the current
hostilities. It is understood, however, that about thirty
German pictures have been brought into "Manohukuo" under
the agreement between the "Manchukuo" Picture Monopoly and
German interests, and that of this number, about twenty
are considered suitable to be brought into Japan. Some,
indeed, are reported to be already in Japan in stook. It
is reported that the Japanese firm of Towa Shoji is to
dietribute about half of these pictures and that a new
company, the Nihon Eiga Boeki Kabushiki Kaisha, organized
for the purpose, is to distribute the remainder (Interview
No. 2).
Censorship Provisions in Law
Article 27 of Ordinance No. 1 provides general rules
for censorehip as follows:
In case where motion pictures that have been
censored in accordance with the provisions of
Artiole 14, Para. 1 of the Motion Pioture Law fall
within the purview of any of the following numbers,
they shall fail to be qualified:-
1.
Pictures that are calculated to debase the
dignity of the Imperial Household or to
injure the prestige of the Empire;
2.
Pictures that are calculated to propagate
ideology in defiance of the Imperial Consti-
tution;
3.

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
3. Piotures that are oalculated to be detri-
mental from the political or military,
diplomatic or economical points of view
and/or from the view point of public
interest;
4.
Piotures that are calculated to defy the
good manners or to injure the national
moral;
5.
Pictures that are calculated to be especially
detrimental to the purity of the national
language;
6.
Pictures the art, technique or technicali-
ties of production of which is particularly
inferior;
7. Pictures that are calculated otherwise to
retard the progress of the national culture.
Censorehip Problem
The censorship problem hae been somewhat compli-
cated by the new law. The first censorship takes place
in the customs house where the customs authorities
review the film and accept it or reject it for importa-
tion. The second censorship provided for in this law
takes place as heretofore in the Bureau of Public Order
of the Ministry of Home Affairs, altnough under certain
ciroumstances the Prefectural Governor may censor. The
Ministry of Education now has acquired certain rights
of selection (see Article 15 of Law No. 66 and Article
35 of Ordinanoe No. 1), and it is not now known by the
film importers whether the Ministry of Home Affairs and
the Ministry of Education will be able to get together
on their censorship or whether a separate censorship
will be conducted by the Ministry of Education (Inter-
view No. 2).
Toho vs. Shochiku
Report No. 59 from this office cited above relates
how the two motion picture groups of Japan, Toho and
Shochiku, endeavored to obtain control of the Nippon
Motion

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
Hotion Picture Company, Limited, usually known as
Nikkatsu. The struggle has continued since that time
with varying fortunes, and it has not been possible to
determine definitely the ownership of the Nikkatsu
stock on account of legal complications. In the mean-
time, Nikkatsu shares which had been quoted at Y21.00
in tne spring of 1939, skyrocketed to ¥92.00. Accord-
ing to newspaper reports, 23,000 shares of Nikkatsu
stock, which would determine the control of Nikkateu
by Toho or Shochiku, are in litigation, and the legal
decision to determine their disposition cannot be
terminated for two or three years.

CONFIDENTIAL
Raw Stook and Prints
There is practically no American cr other 1mported
film available for making prints for distribution in
Japan. Prints for projection are being made on stock
produced by the Fuji Photo Film Company, Limited, which
has its factory near Yokohama. This film is not proving
to be very satisfactory as 1t stretches and breaks
easily. Sound and singing are often badly reproduced.
It is estimated by local distributors that before wear-
ing out, the prints made on locally produced stock will
permit of not over 60 percent of the projections obtain-
able from Eastman stock formerly imported.
Taxation
Some of the American motion picture compenies are
being subjected to an arbitrary method of taxation.
This is true only of those offices located in certain
areas of the city, especislly in the district of the
Kanda-bashi taxation office of Tokyo City. Here, in
order to determine the profit for the purposes of inoome
tax, instead of determining the actual profit from the
accounts of the firm, which seeme to be contemplated by
law, the tax agents have, since the fiscal year 1935-36
to date, arbitrerily assumed that 7 percent of the gross
receipts of the company were profit and have proceeded
to levy income tax on that basis. The companies con-
cerned have protested but as yet nothing has resulted
from their protests.
Distributors

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
-12b -
CONFIDENTIAL
Distributors Pessimistic
Although various extreme measures feared by the
American distributors did not materialize when the
regulations were promulgated, the books and business
of the firme are wholly at the disposal of the Japan-
ese; the reports which must be compiled and rendered
involve much red tape and are very onerous; taxation
is often applied in an arbitrary manner and, of course,
may be increased by law. The foreign firms, moreover,
are obliged to depend greatly on their Japanese
employees and, owing to the loose wording of Japanese
laws and regulations, and the arbitrariness of Japan-
ese police and ministries, shey are, as a matter of
faot, almost completely at the mercy of the Japanese
authorities. Whether the distributors can continue to
operate in the former manner or not will depend on the
number of films which they are permitted to import and
distribute. If permits to import and release amount
to less than a dozen films each per annum, it will
scarcely be possible for these companies to maintain
their offices and continue to do business as hereto-
fore.

Article l. -"Persons who intend to obtain a permit
for motion picture production business under the
provisions of Article 2, Para. 1 of the Motion
Picture Law shall file the Application for a permit
in triplicate, stating therein the partioulars men-
tioned hereunder to the Home Minister and the Educa-

governor competent over the territory where the
principal office of the motion pioture production
business concerned exists (in the prefeoture of
Tokyo, the Direotor of the Metropolitan Police Board,
same ontext following):-
1. Full name and domicile;
2. Soope of business;
3. The place where the motion picture studio exists;
4. The structure, installations and equipment of
the motion pioture studio:-
A. Entire plan of the construction (to attach
the drawings);
B. Construction of the building, installations
and equipments employed for photographing,
sound recording, developing, printing,
projecting and storing (to attach the
drawings);
C. The kinds and number of cameras, recording
and printing machines;
D. Electric installations (to attach drawings).
5. The kinds of motion pictures to be produced;
6. Quantity of motion picture prints to be produced
in a year;
7. Number of employees and their respective kinds
of work;
8. The time when the business is to be commenced.
To the Application for a permit of the preceding
paragraph shall the prospeotus of industry and the
tentative statement of profit and loss acoount be
attached.
When the party intends to alter the particulars of No.2,
No. 3, No. 4, A and/or B, No. 5 or No. 8 of Para. 1
hereof, he shall obtain the permission of the Home
Minister and Eduoation Minister, through the office of

alteration in acoordance with the example of Para. 1
hereof.

the produotion of the paper in proof of the copyright
of the motion picture.
When the motion picture producer has altered the parti-
culars of No. 2 to No. 4 or the principal part of
No. 5 of Para. 1 hereof, notification thereof shall

the latter part of Article 9, Para. 1 of the Motion
Picture Law.
Artiole 16. - The selection and recommendation of

1. Recommendation;
2. Awarding a prize;
The prize of No. 2 of the preceding paragraph shall be

selected from among those to which the Education
Minister

Article 18. - When the Education Minister orders to

If the owner of the motion picture print of the preced-

to the notification of the preceding paragraph, he
shall submit, without delay, a notification stating
the reason therefor to the Education Minister.
Article 19. - What is referred to as foreign motion
pictures in the Motion Picture Law shall be those fall-
ing within the purview of either of the pictures mentioned
hereunder:-
1. The motion pictures produced in a foreign country,
provided, however, that those motion pictures

under the laws of the Japanese Empire and of
which the subject of the Japanese Empire being
the principal representer, performer and/or
cameraman and of whose title and/or the sound
reproduction being not principally foreign language
shall be excluded;
Article 20. - The motion picture distributors shall
not distribute foreign dramatic motion pictures in
exoess of the quantity allotted by the Home Minister.
The allotment of the preceding paragraph shall be
effected in respect of dramatic motion pictures
proposed to be distributed within the next following
year, provided, however, that as to the person who

allotment shall be effeoted with respect to such

Article 2l. - When the motion picture distributors

Article 26. - The motion picture which was censored
in accordance with the provisions of Article 13,
Para. 1 of the Motion Picture Law shall be dealt with
as disqualified if it falls within the purview of
any one of the following numbers:-
1. Piotures that are calculated to debase the
dignity of the Imperial Household or to spoil
the prestige of the Empire;
2. Pictures that are caloulated to be prejudical
to the interest of the Empire from either
political, military, diplomatic or economic
view points or otherwise;
3. Pictures that are calculated to give rise to
misunderstanding as to the national culture;
4. Pictures the art, technique, or technicalities
of production of which are particularly inferior;
5. Pictures that are otherwise unsuitable for export.
Artiole 27. - In case where motion pictures that
have been censored in aocordance with the provisions
of Article 14, Para. 1 of the Motion Picture Law fall
within the purview of any of the following numbers,
they shall fail to be qualified:-
1.

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
1. Pictures that are oaloulated to debase the
dignity of the Imperial Household or to injure
the prestige of the Empire;
2. Piotures that are caloulated to propagate
ideology in defiance of the Imperial Constitution;
3. Pictures that are caloulated to be detrimental
from the political or military, diplomatic or
economical points of view and/or from the view
point of public interest;
4. Piotures that are calculated to defy the good
manners or to injure the national moral;
5. Pictures that are calculated to be especially
detrimental to the purtty of the national
language;
6. Piotures the art, technique or technicalities
of production of which is particularly inferior;
7. Piotures that are calculated otherwise to retard
the progress of the national culture.
Artiole 28. - As for the pictures which passed the

the censorship under the provisions of Article 24,
Para. 2 and of Article 25, Para. 2, the Seal-of-
Passage and mark and number to be otherwise affixed
on the motion picture print may well be omitted.
Article 29. - The passage of censorship of Article
13, Para. 1 of the Motion Picture Law shall be
valid for a period of three months, and the passage
of censorship of Article 14, Para. 1 of the same
Law for a period of three years, provided, however,
that the passage of censorship exercised by the
prefectural governor in accordance with the
provisions of Article 14, Para. 1 of the same Law
shall be valid for a period of three months and the
validity thereof shall not prevail in other prefecture.
Article 30. - In cases where special circumstances
exist, the censorship authorities may, in respect
of the motion pictures censored, designate the term

than that provided for in the preceding Article or

3. When the Censorship-passage-certificate has

In the case of No. 2 of the preceding Paragraph, a

of Article 25, Para. 1 be altered with respect to

Artiole 34. - The censorship authorities shall charge

When deemed necessary from the point of view of public

The fees provided for in Para. 1 hereof shall be paid

Article 35. - The motion pictures that shall be
caused to be exhibited in accordance with the provi-
sions of Article 15, Para. 1 of the Motion Picture
Law shall be such as have been sanctioned by the
Education Minister to be oontributory toward foster-
ing national spirit or enlightening and promoting
the intellect and talent of the nationals, with the
exception of dramatic motion pictures.
The motion picture exhibitors shall exhibit a quantity
of 250 metres or more, at a time, of the motion picture
that has been sanctioned in accordance with the
provisions of the preceding paragraph, provided, how-
ever, that this rule shall not prevail in the case
where the motion picture exhibitor exhibits the prints
in accordance with the provisions of Article 15,
Para. 2 and also where the motion picture which has
been awarded with Recommendation under the rule of
Article l6 are exhibited.
Article 36. - Persons who intend to obtain sanotions
according to the provisions of Para. 1 of the preceding
Article shall file to the Education Minister the
Application for sanction stating therein the parti-
culars mentioned in No. 1 to No. 4 of Article 25,
Para. 1 together with the print of motion picture
calling for the sanotion and two copies of play-book
(one of the two copies containing the Seal-of-Passage
of the Form No. 8).
Artiole 37. - when the sanction has been rendered in
accordance with the provisions of Article 35, Para.
1, the Seal-of-Sanction of the Form No. 9 shall be
affixed on the play-book of the very motion picture
print on which the Seal-of-Passage of the Form No.8
is being affixed.
Article 38. - The term of validity of the sanotion
of Artiole 35, Para. 1 shall be one and same as the

itself upon passage of the censorship.
Article 39.

Article 39. - The Home Minister may, in cases of
spocial circumstances, cancel the sanction granted

been canoelled in accordance with the provisions of
the preceding paragraph, the Seal-of-Sanction affixed
on the playbook of the very motion picture shall be
struck out by erasure.
Artiole 40. - When the Seal-of-Passage has been re-
affixed on the playbook in accordance with the
provisions of Article 32, Para. 1 with respect to
the motion picture which received the sanction of
Article 35, Para. 1, the playbook concerned shall be
without delay produced before the Education Minister
applying for re-affixation of the Seal-of-Sanction
of the Form No. 9.
Article 4l. - when the prefectural governor intends
to cause the motion picture to be exhibited in accord-
ance with the provisions of Article 15, Para. 2 of
the Motion Picture Law, the period of such exhibition
shall be fixed as within one week at a time, provided,
however, that such exhibition shall not be of a period
put together in excess of six weeks per year for one
cinematographic theatre.
Article 42. - The motion picture exhibitor who operates
a permanent cinematographic theatre shall not exhibit
dramatic foreign motion pictures in excess of fifty
prints as aggregated for the whole year and in respect
of a certain cinematographic theatre.
In case where the exhibitor of the preceding paragraph
exhibits a dramatic foreign motion picture that has
been played at the same theatre of his, such picture
shall not be calculated into the number of prints
of the preceding paragraph.
In the event an exhivitor commences the exhibition of
dramatic foreign motion pictures afresh in the middle
of the year, the number of prints provided for in
Para. 1 hereof shall be computed by the number of
months.
Article 43. - The motion picture exhibitor who operates
a permanent cinematographic theatre shall not have
the exhibition lasting beyond three hours at a time,
provided, however, that, in case where the exhibition
is effeoted in accordance with the provisions of
Article 15, Para. 2 of the Motion Picture Law, exten-
sion shall be allowed within a length of time not
exceeding thirty minutes.
Artiole 44. - The motion picture exhibitor or any
other person who exhibits a motion picture otherwise
than for business shall not have the prints projeoted
on the soreen at a speed of over 27.4 metres a minute.
Artiole 45.

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
Article 45. - The motion picture exhibitor or
the one who exhibits the motion picture otherwise
than for business shall not be allowed to exhibit
unless with the projector equipped with the automatic
safety-switches.
Artiole 46. - The motion picture exhibitor or one
who exhibits the motion pictures otherwise than for
business, shall not allow minors under fourteen of
age to be admitted into the place of exhibition with
the exception of the case where the pictures played
are exclusively those passed by censorship in accord-
ance with the provisions of Article 14, Para. 1 and
recognized by the Education Minister (in case of a
picture passed the censorship of the prefectural
governor, the governor concerned) to be harmless
from minor's educational point of view, provided,
however, that this rule shall not prevail where the
minors under six of age are in company with a guardian.
Article 47. - When the recognition of the preceding
Article has been granted, the Seal-of-Recognition
after the Form No. 10 shall be affixed on the play-
book containing the Seal-of-Passage of the Form No.8,
provided, however, that in the case of the prefectural
governor's recognition such seal may well be omitted.
The provisions of Article 38 to Article 40 shall
apply to the recognition of the preceding Article
with necessary modifications.
Article 48. - The motion picture exhibitor or the
one who exhibits the motion picture otherwise than
for business shall not allow to operate the projeo-
tion machine by other than those with projection
license granted by the prefectural governor, provided,
however, that this rule shall not prevail where the
film concerned is non-inflammable. The motion
picture exhibitor or one who exhibits the motion
picture otherwise than for business shall instal
two sets of projection machines, and, in case the
exhibition lasts as a rule six hours consecutively,
three, and in case of loss hours, two, projection
licensees shall be put to work.
Artiole 49. - In case the full name and domicile shall
be entered in the Application or in the Notification
according to the provisions of this Ordinance, the
trade-name, the sddress of the principal office, the
full name and domicile of the representative thereof
shall be entered in the case of the party concerned
being a juridical person.
Article 50. - Persons who contravened the rules of
Article 3; 4; 11, Para. 1 and of Article 33, shall
be subject to a minor fine.
Article 51.

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
Article 51. - In case his agents, head of his
family, member of his family, inmate, employee or
whosoever works under him shall have committed an
act falling within the purview of the provisions
of the preceding Article, the motion picture producer,
distributor or one owning the motion picture that
has passed the censorship shall not be exempt from
punishment on the excuse that that particular act
had not been in accordance with his direction.
Article 52. - The criminal provisions of Article 50
shall be applied to the member of the board, the
director or any other officer who executes the
affairs in case the offender is a juridical person,
and to the legal agent in case the offender is a
minor or a person incompetent of managing the property,
provided, however, that this rule shall not prevail
where the minor concerned is legally competent same
as an adult in respect of the business concerned.
ADDITIONAL PROVISIONS
Article 53. - This Ordinance shall be enforced as
from the day on which the Motion Picture Law takes
effect, provided, however, that the provisions of
Article 20, Article 42 and Article 46 shall take
effect as from January 1st l940, those of Article
35, Para. 2 as from January 1st 1940 so far as the
cities of Tokyo, Kyoto, Osaka, Yokohama, Kobe and
Nagoya are concerned and as from July 1st 1940 so
far as any other cities, towns or villages are con-
cerned, and that the provisions of Article 45 and 48
shall take effect as from October 1st 1940.
Article 54. - The Home Office Ordinances mentioned
hereunder shall be abolished:-
1. "The motion picture film censorship regulations":
the Home Office Ordinance No. 10 of May 1925;
2. "The export motion picture film regulations":
the Home Office Ordinance No.63 of October 1935;
3. "In the matter concerning the hours of exhibition
of the motion picture and the limitation to the
meterage of the print": the Home Office
Ordinance No. 55 of December 1937.
Artiole 55. - Persons engaging in the business accord-
ing to the provisions of Paragraph 2 of the Addigion-
al Provisions to the Motion Picture Law shall, within
one month as from the day of enforcement of this
Ordinance, send in the notification in duplicate,
stating therein the particulars of No. 1 to No. 3
and No. 5 of Article 1, Para. 1 in respect of the
motion pioture producers; and the particulars of No.l
to No.3 and No.5 of Article 2, Para. 1 in respeot of
the motion picture distributors, to the Home Minister
and the Education Minister through the office of the
prefecgural governor mentioned in Article 1, Para. 1
or

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
or Article 2, Para. 1 as the case may be, provided,
however, that this rule shall not prevail where
the Application for the permit has been meanwhile
filed in accordance with the provisions of Article
1 or Article 2 as the case may be.
Article 56. - The term of validity of the censored
films that have been censored by the administrative
authorities prior to enforcement of this ordinance
or the film which passed the inspection for export
shall be the remainder of the term of validity as
governed by the old rules.
Article 57. - The motion picture exhibitors shall
not be allowed to exhibit motion pictures, within
the interval of six months as from the time of the
provisions of Article 35, Para. 2 taking effect,
with the exception of the number of days equivalent
to fifty percent of the playdays of each month, as
according to the rules of the said paragraph.
In cases where the motion picture exhibitors exhibit
films that have passed the censorship of the
administrative authorities prior to this Ordinance
having taken effect or such duplicates thereof as
having received the sanction of Article 35, Para. 1,
the provisions concerning meterage as mentioned in
said Article Para. 2 shall not prevail during the
interval of three years as from the time when this
Ordinance has taken effect.
Article 58. - In cases where censored films that
have passed the censorship of the administrative
authorities prior to this ordinance coming into
effect are either exclusively exhibited or else
along with the motion pictures that have received
sanction under the rule of Article 46 hereof, with
the exception of the case where the Education
Minister regards it specifically necessary, the
rules of Article 46 shall not prevail.

molosure No. 2 to Report No. 74 from the Tokyo
Consulate General on the subject, "New Regulations
for Motion Pictures in Japan", dated December 5, 1939.
TRANSLATION OF IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 668 ENTITLED,
"MOTION PICTURE LAW ENFORCEMENT ORDINANCE", PRO-
MULGATED ON SEPTEMBER 26, 1939, AND PUBLISHED IN THE
OFFICIAL GAZETTE, EDITION NO. 382O OF SEPTEMBER 27,
1939, TRANSLATION HAVING BEEN MADE IN THIS CONSULATE
GENERAL.
Article 1. - "The motion picture producing business"
of Article 2 of the Motion Picture Law is hereby
defined to be such business as (a) planning motion
pictures, (b) photographing and (c) editing, under
combined management in manufacturing motion pictures.
Such business involving either one or two of
(a) planning motion pictures, (b) photographing
motion pictures, (o) editing motion pictures, shall
be regarded as "motion picture producing business".
The motion picture distributing business" of
Article 2 of the Motion Picture Law is hereby defined
to be such business as (1) renting or (a) selling
motion pictures to (a) motion picture exhibiting
enterprises or (b) other parties exhibiting motion
pictures.
Article 2. - The competent ministers funotioning
under Item one of Article 2, Article 4 and Article
18 of the Motion Picture Law, shall be the Minister
of Home Affairs and the Minister of Education;
The competent minister functioning under Article
5, Article 6, Article 9, Article 12, Item two of
Article 13, and Article 16 of the Motion Picture
Law, shall be the Minister of Home Affairs; and
The competent minister functioning under Article
10, Article il and Item one of Article 15 of the
Motion Picture Law, shall be the Minister of Educa-
tion.
APPENDIX REGULATIONS
These present regulations shall be enforoed as
from the date of enforcement of the Motion Pioture Law.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DATE: January
Restriotions in Japan upon the Importation of American
Films and upon Exchange Remittances
1940 JAN 18 PM 4

Copy sent to Joky
January
1-1498
Colonel Herron called at the Department in order to
discuss the quota which was recently imposed upon the
importation of foreign films into Japan and to press fur-
ther the case which he presented in his letter of Decem-
ber 14, 1939, (file no. 894.4061 Motion P1ctures/32).
During recent years American distributors have imported
into Japan approximately 250 pictures annually. Now, the
total quota for foreign pictures has been fixed at 120
most of which has been allotted to American producers.
FILED
Colonel Herron states that American distributors cannot
16
quota; that the Japanese Government obviously 1s trying
F/N
to drive out American distributors from Japan; that
Japan

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Japan needs American pictures but it seeks, as it did in
"Manohukuo", to have the distribution purely in Japanese
hands; and that American motion pioture distributors will
probably withdraw from Japan rather than turn their films
over to Japanese distributors.
In arguing that this Government should assist Ameri-
can motion picture distributors in coping with the
situation in Japan, Colonel Herron referred several times
to the "agreement" between the Japanese Ministry of
Finance and the American Motion Picture Association of
Japan. He seemed to think that the Department should make
representations, either formal or informal, to the Japanese
Government asking that that "agreement" be not only
carried out but extended.
Incidentally, Colonel Herron said that he and his
associates have discovered that Mr. Hisaji Kubo, the
Japanese who "negotiated" the "agreement" between the
association and the Japanese Ministry of Finance, is a
"crook" and the association has ceased dealing with him.
Mr. Jones stated that consideration would be given
in the Department to instructing Ambassador Grew to en-
deavor informally to obtain an enlargement of the quota
for American pictures and that in this connection a full
statement from Colonel Herron of the facts in connection
w1th

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
with the quota allotment, the previous business done by
American distributors, and the probable effects of the
operation of the new quota, would be helpful to the De-
partment. In reply to the suggestion of Mr. Jones that
in view of the financial situation in Japan it might be
difficult to convince the Japanese Government that a
higher quota should be allowed, Colonel Herron said that
the American distributors appreciate fully the financial
situation in Japan and that they would not insist upon
the remission from Japan to the United States of funds
accruing from the showing of the films in Japan.
Colonel Herron indicated his intention of submitting
to the Department a full statement of the facts.
Colonel Herron at first urged that the Department
endeavor through informal action by Ambassador Grew to
persuade the Japanese Government to live up to the
"agreement" above cited and to extend that "agreement".
Meesrs. Ballantine and Jones suggested that in view of
the character of that "agreement" it was doubtful that
the

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.94)
-4
the Department would be willing to proceed upon that
basis but that consideration would be given to the
matter. Subsequently Colonel Herron said that he would
send a statement to the Department which, without
reference to the previous "agreement", would give suf-
ficient facts to enable the Department to take some
action in the matter purely on the basis of reduced
quota allotment for American films. Colonel Herron
seemed satisfied with this arrangement.

Dear Mr. Mackay:
With further reference to our conversations when I was in
Washington last week with Mr. Ballantine and Mr. Jones regarding
894.4O61 MOTiON PiCTURES/39
the new decree in Japan limiting the number of foreign pictures
to be allowed to be imported there, the motion picture industry
would appreciate it very much indeed if instructions could be
forwarded by the State Department to our Embassy in Japan request-
ing that as vigorous a protest as possible be made to the Japanese
Government against this new decree.
The decree limits the number of foreign pictures to be im-
ported into Japan to 120 in 1940, allotting the American com-
panies as follows:
Paramount
14
Metro
13
Columbia
11
United Artists
7
and the other four American distributors 9 each.
The Japanese independent distributors who handle not only
foreign pictures but Japanese are to be given permits to import
39 foreign pictures, and two Japanese exhibitors are given 5 per-
mits each for foreign pictures, allowing these latter to import
direct rather than through distributors by virtue of the fact
that they are allowed foreign import permits.
This allocation was a very severe blow to our industry andi
we feel that it is the first move made by the Japanese Govern-
ment in their efforts to try and force our companies to discon
tinue the operation of their own offices in Japan, compelling them
目
to sell their product to domestic distributors. This, of cours
is the general trend of all businesses in Japan, which we realize,
but the American motion picture distributors feel that they know
best how to distribute their product and all of the large American
companies have constantly refused to allow their product to be
handled by any but their own direct agents.
F/N
The limitation of the number of pictures allowed these
different companies is made with the direct view of putting such

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Mr. Raymond C. Mackay,
January 10, 1940
companies out of business because it is impossible, even with the
number of 14 allotted, for the large companies to carry on their
business there and pay their overhead expenses. The Japanese
Government, of course, knows this and have passed this decree
with that in mind.
Unfortunately, it seems the only way to get anywhere near
a fair deal from the present government in Japan is to constantly
bear down on them by the efforts of our Embassy.
Again thanking you for your kindness in taking this matter
up for our industry, and with best personal wishes, I am,
Most sincerely,

Reforence Tokyo's despatch no. 4733,
ay 51, 1940, entitled "Restriotions on
Importations of American Moving Piotures".
It will be recalled that in January of
thim year Colonel Herron of the Motion
Pieture Producers and Distributors of
America, Inoorporated, approached the
Department for assistance in regard to
difficulty which American distributors
were having in importing into Japan
American moving pictures and in remitting
to the United States their profits.
The Embassy reports that the diffi-
culties of the American distributors have
not been resolved; that the Embassy has
been in close and frequent touoh with the
distributors; but that assistance by the
Embassy untii recently was virtually im-
possible beoause the distributors stood
by the position that they had conoluded
an "agreement" with the Japanese Govern-
ment by which the latter would permit
the importation over a specified period
of a specified number of American motion
pictures. It will be recalled that the
validity of this "agreement" is very much
in doubt.
The

The Embassy informed the distributors
that it could not take action on the basis

whioh the competent Japanese authorities
"expeoted to follow". Nevertheless the

could not lay the plans for future opera-
tions without knoiing the intentions of

aoquiesced in that suggestion and the

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's con-
UL 16 1940
fidential instruction no. 1887 dated January 31, 1940
FILED
(894.4061 Motion Pictures/36), with which there were
enclosed copies of papers relating to the restrictions
imposed
F/N

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
imposed in Japan on the importation of American moving
pictures and on the remitting to the United States of
profits in Japan of American moving picture distributors.
The Embassy has been in close and frequent touch with
the American distributors, but until very recently the
position taken by the American distributors was such that
intervention by the Embassy on their behalf would not
have been expected to serve any useful purpose. Their
position was, in short, that they had concluded an "agreement*
with the Japanese Government by which the latter would
permit the importation over a specified period of a specified
number of American motion pictures. In the memorandum of
conversation on January 3, 1940, between officers of the
Department and Colonel Herrron, a copy of which is enclosed
with the Department's instruction under reference, there
occurs the statement by Colonel Herron that "he and his
associates have discovered that Mr. Hisaji Kubo, the Japanese
who 'negotiated the agreement' between the association
and the Japanese Ministry of Finance, is a crook' and
the association has ceased dealing with him. If one can
assume that Colonel Herron was fully apprised of the
extended dealings which the local distributors had with
Kubo, the conclusion is inescapable that Colonel Herron
was something less than entirely frank with the Department.
Mr. A. E. Daff, Far Eastern Supervisor of Universal
Pictures (Japan), informed us, that the distributors'
association had several interviews with Kubo to agree on
the terms on which he (Kubo) would undertake to procure
the "agreement" from the Department of Finance, that Kubo
originally demanded Yen 100,000 which, he claimed, would
1eave

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
leave him little after he had crossed the palm of the
Minister of Finance, but that he had finally agreed to
accept Yen 30,O00. The distributors were quite aware
from the beginning that they were dealing with a most
unsavory character, for they went to the trouble of
taking a stenographic record of each of their interviews
with Kubo, and the statement of Colonel Herron (assuming
that he was aware of the dealings of his associates in
Japan) that Kubo was "discovered" to be a "crook", con-
sorts poorly with the facts.
In any event, as it was clear to us that the hands
of the distributors were none too clean, we informed the
distributors that the Embassy could not make representations
to the Japanese Government on the basis of a document
which, on its face, was not an agreement but merely an
indication of a policy which the competent Japanese
authorities "expected" to follow with regard to permitting
the importation of films over a stated period. On the
other hand, the Embassy stated to the distributors that
we realized that the failure of the Japanese Government
to indicate what its policy would be after the expiration
of the stated period, would effectively prevent the
distributors from laying out plans for their future opera-
tions in this country, and that the Embassy was prepared
to roquest the Japanese Government that the latter clarify
its position in the matter. After considerable hesitation
and delay, the distributors expressed desire that the
Embassy intervene along the lines above indicated. Ac-
cordingly, there was delivered to the Foreign Office on
May

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
May 25 a note, with memorandum annexed, a copy of which
is enolosed.
Mr. Daff has expressed, on behalf the distributors,
appreciation for the Embassy's cooperation.
The character of the means taken by the distributors
to procure the so-called "agreement" will be appreciated
when it is realized that the Kubo with whom they dealt is
the Kubo who is the subject of the Department's instruction
no. 1740, June 1, 1939.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch

The American Ambassador to the Japanese Minister for For-

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 1556.
The American Ambassador presents his compliments
to His Excellency the Minister for Foreign Affairs and

the position of American distributors in Japan of motion

Mr. Grew would appreciate an early and sympathetic

enclosed memorandum.
Tokyo, May 25, 1940.

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
On August 18, 1937, a committee representing the
rican motion pioture companies operating in Japan was
informed verbally by an official of the Finance Ministry
that henceforth import permits would be required for all
foreign films imported into the country. On September 22,
the film companies were notified through the press that
the Finance authorities had decided to ban totally the
importation of all foreign films for the balance of the
year and the Finance authorities were also quoted as
having stated that the pan cn film imports "would probably
extend well into 1938".
Acting on instructions from their respective head
offices, the American film companies opened negotiations
with the Finance authorities in February, 1938, for an
alleviation of the ban on film imports. In July the
negotiations had progressed so satisfactorily that the
film companies were advised by the Finance Ministry to
make arrangements to have films shipped to Japan in order
that they could be cleared immediately on the issuance of
film import permits.
On Ootober 3, 1938, the American film companies were
informed, through their association, the American Motion
Picture Distributors Association of Japan, that the Finance
Ministry was prepared to consider the issuance of permits
for the importation of $30,000 worth of film prints and
between October 15, 1938 and December 31, 1938, permits
were actually granted for approximately $10,000 worth of
film prints. During l939, the Finance authorities issued
further permits for American films, the total value of
tho

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
the permits issued during the period frem Oetober 15,
1938, to the end of December, 1939, amounting to
19,792.16.
On March 18, 1940, the Finance authorities issued
permits for the importation of $1,4ll.94 worth of films
but since that date no further permits have been issued.
During the past two months the American film companies
have made repeated requests to the Finance authorities for
some indication as to when they may expect some action on
their outstanding applications for further permits. So
far they have been unable to obtain assurances that addi-
tional permits will be forthcoming in the immediate future.
As the American film companies are unable to make
definite operating plans for the future pending some indi-
cation on the part of the Finance authorities as to their
intentions regarding the issuance of film import permits,
they have requested the Embassy to endeavor to ascertain
from the Finance authorities, through the Foreign Office,
some indication as to when they may expect action on their
outstanding applications.

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
THE MOTION PICTURE INDUSTRY IN JAPAN - 1939
Activities of various motion picture enterprises as well as
the normal growth of the film industry in Japan were greatly handicapped
during the year 1939 due mainly to a very serious shortage of raw stocks.
This shortage is the result of
(1) Strict enforcement of exchange regulations which
seriously curtailed the supply of imported raw
film stocks.
(2) The inability of local manufacturers of raw films
to boost their production to an extent to completely
relieve the shortage.
Perhaps the most important development in the Japanese motion
picture industry during 1939 was the passage of the Motion Picture Law.
The detailed regulations governing the enforcement of the law comprise
58 articles, most of which were put into effect on October 1, the balance
becoming effective January 1, 1940.
The prime objective of the law is rigid control over production.
distribution, and exhibition for purposes of cultural advancement and of
promoting the sound development of Japan's motion picture industry.
Domestic producers are required to submit their scenarios for approval
prior to the time actual work is begun on the film. The showing of
cultural films will be compulsory and the industry is being utilized
for propagation of national ideals and thought. The distribution and
exhibition of foreign films will be greatly restricted, no theater being
allowed to exhibit more than 50 new foreign features a year.
The estimated import of raw stocks from foreign countries is
not more than 10% of the annual import before the *Incident". Raw
stocks are chiefly imported from America and Germany. Locally manufactured
raw

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
一2 -
raw stocks available to the industry consist of moderate speed panchromatic
negative, black and white positive, and sound recording positive. There
are no duplicating stocks mamufactured in Japan and because of shortages
of this sound recording positive and in some instances regular positive
have been employed for preparation of duplicate negatives.
The partial lifting of the ban on importation of foreign films
in October, 1938 has supplied this country with about 300 foreign pictures
including shorts by the end of 1939. During the year 1939 146 foreign
pictures were released in Japan.
Of the foreign pictures, American productions accounted for
114 films. This is a 10% increase over the same period of 1938, but
is only 42% of the films released during the one year previous to the
"Incident".
Approximately 35 German pictures were imported into Japan during
the year 1939. The major portion of these pictures were imported from
Manchukuo through the Manchukuoan Film Monopoly which had acquired the
pictures under a German-Manchukuo Trade Agreement. Only 10 German
productions were, however, reported to have been released during the year.
Other European pictures released during the year were 10 British, 10
French, and 2 Italian.
Although the shortage of raw stocks has hindered the normal
growth of the motion picture industry in Japan, the meterage that has gone
through censorship has been annually increasing. The total meters
censored during 1939 has been reported as 29,664,000. This is a 122%
increase over the sams period of l938. This increase in censored
meterage is due to the strict economy of film practiced by all producers
and the sudden inerease in the produetion of cultural films as well as
continued high production of newsreels and educational pietures.
During 1935 535 domestic productions were released in Japan.
Th1

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
This represents a drop of about 20% in the number of produetion when
compared with the average for the previous three years. Fully 50% of
the produetions are still of the historical or classical type while the
present Sino-Japanese Incident has offered some excellent themes for
modern type pictures and has been the source of some of the production
of the year.
With the installation of sound recording systems by two
producers during the year, all major studios are now producing sound
films. Expansion and building projects by domestic producers have been
dormant during the year due to rigid control over building materials
exercised by the government. Due to the difficulty of importing modern
equipment the studios in Japan are encountering much difficulty in re-
placing their old cameras and other studio equipment.
At the beginning of the year there were 1,885 theaters in
Japan. With few exceptions these are predominantly small buildings.
No large theaters were constructed during the year because of rigid
control over building materials. More than 90% of theaters are wired
for sound, the greater portion of which being locally manufactured systems.
There has been a noticeable penetration of Japanese motion
pioture interests into Manchukuo and China. Production and distribution
of motion pictures in Manchukuo is controlled by Manchukuoan Film
Monopoly which is under the guidance of Japanese erecutives and technicians.
No definite figures are available on their production, but it is estimated
that eight to ten major productions were completed during 1939. During
the year 497 Japanese films were exported to Manchukuo. A large number
of Japanese films are also being exported to China, particularly into
the northern parts where many theaters are under the control of Japanese
interests.

October 8, 1940.
Yokohama's 708, September 10,
1940, Importation of American News
Films to be Stopped.
The Consul reports that after
October 2, 1940, foreign news films
will not be permitted in Japan.
The Consul at Yokohama has long
favored the stopping by us of the
importation into the United States of
Japanese news films. During the year
July 1, 1939 to June 30, 1940,
198,391 feet of Japanese news film
was invoiced at Yokohama at a value
of $8,699 for importation into the
United States.
Comment: The present temper of
the American public would appear to
be such that any intended effect of
Japanese news reels unfavorable to
the United States when shown in this
country would be largely neutralized,
and that by consequence measures to
prevent importation are unnecessary.

Dear Mr. Hamilton:
In reference to your letter of December 3lst regarding
the message received through our Embassy in Tokio from the
representatives of our companies there, we discussed this
at length here at a Foreign Managers' meeting of the repre-
sentatives of the companies and it was decided that it was a
matter that each company would have to take care of indi-
vidually, that it was not a group proposition.
Some com-
panies did not have their money in the National City Bank
and others felt that they could straighten this matter out
to their own satisfaction in their own way and don't care to
expose their hands to the other companies.
The case is a little bit unusual but I presume the tre-
mendous sums of frozen money that our companies have piled
up in Tokio over a period of three or four years has resulted
in a condition which the banks do not like.
any thanks for your kindness in passing this message
on to us, and with best personal wishes for the New Year, I

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 6, 1941.
According to Tokyo's 148, January 31,
6 p.m., the Paramount motion picture firm in
Tokyo has been "definitely instructed to in-
vest in Japanese national bonds". The Embassy
feels that, if one firm takes this step, added
pressure will be exerted on other American
pioture concerns and all American concerns
having funds frozen in Japan. The Embassy
suggests that the Department may wish to
emphasize to the Paramount company its
policy regarding the purchase of Japanese
national bonds.
It is suggested that Mr. Hamilton tele-
phone to Colonel Brederick L. Herron in New
York, with whom we have had contact in the
past, and reemphasize this Department's policy
with respect to the purchase of Japanese na-
tional bonds by American firms and discuss
with him whether Colonel Herron may appropri-

Dear Mr. lamilton:
I just have your letter of February l3th relative to the
investment of Paramount's representative in Japanese National
Bonds.
At the time this matter was brought up the Foreign Managers
all agreed here that the matter of how the companies invested
their monies was something for the individual company to handle,
and while they appreciated advice on the matter, they thought
that nothing should be done as a group. As a result of that
14
meeting I know that different companies sent out instructions
to their representatives in Japan, most of which were guided by
the information contained in your letter of December 31, 1940.
In the case of Paramount I don't imagine that they placed
very much money in the purchase of these Japanese bonds and pro-
bably only did so upon pressure which they felt they could not
resist as it has been impossible a great deal of the time to
deal with the Japanese overnment in regard to our product there
because of the fact that they did not live up to their agreements
made at different times with our companies.

which was just received by you.

PARAMOUNT PICTURES INC.

March 21, 1941
Gov. Carl E. Milliken,
Motion Picture Prod. & Distr. of America, Inc.,
New York, N. Y.
My dear Governor Milliken:
Your letter of arch 20th plus the enclosure of copy of
letter dated March 17th from Mr. Maxwell M. Hamilton to Colonel
Herron has been received. Herewith the chronology of the pur-
chase by Paramount Films, Limited, of Japan, of the Japanese bonds
referred to:
Some time last year, we became interested in the purchase of
Japanese bonds. As you know, our funds in Japan have been blocked,
and an attractive feature of these bonds was the fact that the three
and one-half percent interest was remittable in dcllars. There was
a further attractive feature in assurance we received from Japan
that through the purchase of these bonds in Japan, there would be
an easement of the blocking of our funds in Japan; and further sums
in dollars could be remitted from Japan.
On ecember 26, we received a cable from our r. Perkins, indi-
cating to us that it would be advantageous at that time, to purchase
the bonds up to about eighty percent of the present and accumulating
cash, and on January third, we gave our consent by cable.
On February 14, we received a letter from Major Herron to
which was attached a copy of a letter dated February 13 from Mr.
Maxwell M. Hamilton, to Colonel Herron, in which he stated that on
December 24, the Enbassy at Tokyo advised the representatives of the
American film companies that the epartment of State would not look
with favor on any American concerns investing its funds in Japanese
bonds.
This news came to us as a complete surprise, and we immediately
cabled to Japan to cancel any further bond purchases, and shortly
thereafter we wrote to ascertain why we were not apprised of the
viewpoint expressed by the State Department. We are currently
awaiting an answer to this communication. Naturally, a good deal
of this difficulty could have been avoided by the State Department's
contacting this office directly.
Naturally, we are complying, and intend to comply with the
request of the State Department, but there are large sums of money
involved, and I think it is necessary for us to receive a further
clarification of this matter.
We would like to know, if it is possible, why this type of
investment is not approved, especially in view of the fact that it

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
enables us not only to receive in dollars the interest on the bonds,
but also an unnamed amount of dollars. If we did not purchase these
Japanese bonds, we would not get any dollars out of Japan, whereas
through the purchase of these bonds, we are enabled to receive fairly
favorable in two senses of the word, the first being to our own busi-
ness; and the second in that it depletes the gold reserve of the
Government that at the moment, appears unfriendly to our own. Would
it be possible for you to obtain for us ambetter understanding of
the issues involved, from the viewpoint of the American Government?
With kind regards,
Sincerely,
(sgd) John W. Hicks, Jr.

RCM
Tokyo's despatch no. 5462, March 24, 1941:
"Purchase of Japanese National Bonds by
American Motion Picture Concerns".
The Ambassador reports that the representa-
tives of American motion picture companies met
on several occasions with the Commercial
Attache and that particular care was taken to
impress upon the representatives the attitude
of the Department and of the Embassy in regard
to the investment of American funds in Japanese
national bonds.
On January 31 Mr. Jonas Albeck, repre-
sentative of Paramount Films Limited, indicated
that he was seriously considering investing
funds of his company in Japanese national
bonds (Albeck is a Danish subject and re-
portedly owns property in Japan). The trans-
action was held in abeyance pending the
arrival of Mr. Albeck's power of attorney.
The Commercial Attache emphasized the danger
of purchase of bonds by one concern in that
it would "open the way" for added Japanese
pressure on all other American companies
having frozen funds in Japan (such funds
total

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
approximately 45 million Yen). On March 7
Mr. Albeck called the Commercial Attache, as
he had promised to do, and stated that he had
ordered an unspecified amount of Japanese
Government bonds, as instructed by Mr. J. E.
Perkins, Far Eastern Manager for the Paramount
coTpany. (Press rparted Oaramount purchased 476s,000)
Mr. Perkins called on Mr. Williams on
March 18 and discussed this question with him.
Mr. Williams emphasized the embarrassing
position in which other American motion picture
companies were placed, the fact that Paramount
was directly assisting Japan to finance her
war and that publicity regarding the purchase
by Paramount of Japanese national bonds had
appeared on the day following the passing of
the Lease-Lend Bill. Mr. Perkins stated
confidentially that his company had obtcined
a guarantee from the Minister of Finance for
a permit to remit to the United States ten
per cent of the value of the bonds purchased,
an agreement that exchange permits would be
granted for the annual interest payments on
the bonds, and had insisted on a clause
binding the Minister of Finance to repurchase
the bonds at the purchase price on seven days'
notice. He expressed personal regret that
his company's representative had acted arainst
the Embassy's advice and added no further
purchases were intended.
Mr.

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
JIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
Mr. M. Shathin, local manager of Warner
Brothers, informed the Commercial Attache' on
March 17 the Mitsui Trust Company had approached
him with the request that frozen funds (approxi-
mately eight million yen) of all American
motion picture companies in Japan be deposited
with that trust comoany for five years. The
Mitsui company agreed to allow withdrawal
at any time of funds for which the Ministry
of Finance would grant an exchange permit,
and offered to pay interest from 3.8% to
3.9% per annum should deposits exceed one
million yen. The Mitsui company offered to
provide a written guarantee covering the
transfer to the United States of interest
on the funds deposited. Mr. Williams suggested
a guarantee be obtained from the Ministry of
Finance regarding exchange permits for
remission of annual interest. The Embassy
considers the Mitsui offer as significant
and probably indicating shortage of "licuid"
cash. The Embassy perceives no objection
to the Mitsui offer.

2
the purchase of Japanese bonds by American motion

The Commercial Attache has had several
meetings with representatives of American motion
picture companies here, and on each occasion has
