Japanese national bonds.
On January 31, 1941, a group of these
motion picture gentlemen called on Mr. Williams for
a further discussion of the matter. At that time
only one firm, namely, Paramount Films Ltd., was
seriously considering such a step. The representative
of this firm, Mr. Jonas Albeck, is a Danish subject
and reportedly owns some property in Japan. He has
been with the Paramount Company only a few months
being formerly with the Columbia Pictures.
Mr. Albeck stated that his predecessor
had made a definite proposition to his New York head-
quarters as far back as November, 1940, and had
eventually obtained approval of the bond purchase
plan. In early January this year a proposal was
laid before the Japanese Government, which was
accepted around the middle of January, but the deal
was held in abeyance until Mr. Albeck's power of
attorney

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
3
attorney had been received from New York and arrange-
ments made for him to sign checks.
At this meeting Mr. Williams explained in
detail to Mr. Albeck and the other motion picture
men present the position taken by the Department
and this Embassy in regard to American companies in-
vesting in Japanese national bonds and stressed the
danger of such action on the part of one concern to
all American interests in Japan, namely, that it
would open the way for added pressure from the
Japanese on, not only other American picture com-
panies who have upwards of eight million yen deposited
in Japan, but also on all American firms having
funds frozen here totalling around Yen 45 million.
Mr. Albeck promised to keep the Commercial Attache
informed as to his actions in connection with the
purchase of bonds.
On the basis of the above conference we
despatched to the Department our telegram #148,
January 3lst, 6 p.m.
On March 7, 1941, Mr. Albeck called alone
to see Mr. Williams in accordance with his promise
of January 3lst. He stated that he had received
his power of attorney and also definite instruc-
tions from Mr. J. E. Perkins, Far Eastern Manager
for Paramount, to proceed with the purchase of the
bonds and he had, therefore, placed his order on
March

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
4
March 6, 1941, for an unspecified amount of these
bonds. This information was cabled to the Depart-
ment on March 7, 6 p.m., our #371.
On March 18th Mr. J. E. Perkins, Far
Eastern Manager for Paramount, was in Tokyo en
route to the United States on home leave. He called
on me in connection with the purchase of Japanese
bonds and was referred to Mr. Williams who went over
the whole matter with him and emphasized the em-
barrassing position in which other American motion
picture companies had been placed by his company's
action. It was also pointed out that his company
was directly assisting Japan to finance her war
program with American funds. He was shown a news-
1/
paper clipping, attached hereto, from the Japan
Times and Advertiser of March 13, 1941, morning
edition, reproducing on the front page a 32" x 43"
photograph of Mr. Albeck and his Japanese assistant
with the explanation that these men were responsible
for the Paramount Film Company's purchase of
Yen 765,O00 worth of Japanese Government securities.
It was also pointed out to Mr. Perkins that this
publicity appeared in Japanese papers on the day
following the passing by the United States Congress
of the Lease Lend Bill.
Mr. Perkins informed Mr. Williams con-
fidentially that the terms of the agreement made
with

=== Page 190 === (confidence 1.00)
5
with the Japanese by his company included a
guarantee from the Minister of Finance that an ex-
change permit be granted for an amount equal to
10% of the value of bonds purchased and that ex-
change permits would be granted covering the
annual interest payments on the bonds. He stated
that there was also a clause in the agreement bind-
ing the Minister of Finance to re-purchase these
bonds at the price paid any time on seven days'
notice. Mr. Perkins expressed his personal regret
that his company's representative had acted against
the advice of this Embassy and stated that no
further purchases of bonds were intended.
In this connection Mr. M. Shathin, local
manager of Warner Brothers, called on the Conmercial
Attache on March 17th and informed him that he had
been approached by the Mitsui Trust Company with
the request that all the American motion picture
companies deposit their frozen funds with the
Mitsui Trust Company for a period of five years.
The amount of such funds is around eight million
yen, the bulk of which is now in the Tokyo branch
of the National City Bank of New York. The Trust
Company stipulated that in case the Ministry of
Finance allows the remittance of any part or the
whole of these funds to the United States, they
may be withdrawn at any time for that purpose. The
offer

=== Page 191 === (confidence 1.00)
6
offer was also made that if the deposits exceed
one million yen, interest would be paid at the
rate of from 3.8% to 3.9% per annum, and that ex-
change permits would be granted to transfer this
interest to the United States semi-annually. The
Trust Company indicated its willingness to provide
a letter of guarantee covering the transfer of
this interest. Mr. Williams suggested that such
a letter be obtained from the Ministry of Finance
rather than from the Trust Company.
This proposition originating from the
Mitsui Trust Company is considered significant in-
asmuch as it very probably reflects the shortage of
liquid cash.
From an investment standpoint this offer
appears much more attractive than the purchase of
national bonds and one to which this Embassy could
offer no objection inasmuch as the National City
Bank has informed American motion picture concerns
that it is transferring their deposits to the Bank
of Japan and has requested the companies to sign a
release limiting the responsibility of the bank
to the assets of this branch.
Mr. Shathin is to keep Mr. Williams advised
as to any decision reached in connection with this
proposition from the Mitsui Trust Company.
Respectfully yours;
Joseph C.Grew.

RAW FILM ALLOTMENTS
Early in January the authorities announced that
the supply of raw film furnished to consumers other than those
engaged in the production of cultural pictures would be re-
duced by approximately 36% as compared with 1940. Allowances
for the first three months were supposed to be based on the
amount of film previcusly used by the individual companies,
but since no announcement was made as to the total footage
consumed by Japanese firms during the previous year, there
is no method of ascertaining whether the American distri-
buting companies are being discriminated against or not.
The total amount of film released for the three months period
was scheduled to be 12,150,000 metric feet. The American
distributing companies each received about 10,000 metric
feet per month.
Complete details of the plans for the April-June
period are not yet available. Some changes in the quotas
for individual companies may be made in view of the new policy
whereby producers of superior pictures will be given pre-
ference over the others. This plan may be used as a method
of discrimination against the American concerns, but there
1s no evidence that this is intended. One press report
stresses the fact that so far as imported pictures are
concerned, the American companies have been considered as
most important, but that hereafter the Japanese importers
should no longer be treated as secondary in this field.
It

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
3.
It is reported that the Japan Sensitizing Materials
Manufacturing Association will handle the allocation of raw
film under the direction of the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry. Although it is generally assumed that the total
April-June allowances will be approximately the same as the
preceding quarter, a report in the March issue of "Diamond"
states that the total output of raw film in Japan is now
7,500,000 metric feet per month, of which 5,000,000 feet 1s
to be used for the production of military, cultural and educa-
tional pictures. If this statement is accurate, the monthly
allotments to all other users of rew film will be reduced
from 4,050,000 feet to 2,500,000 feet. The Dome1 News
Agenoy, in reporting a recent äiscussion among the officials
concerned with this question, stated on April 2nd that
"there will be no big difference" in the second quarter
quotas. The officials referred to presumably are the
members of the "Movie Film Distribution Control Council" which
1s composed of representatives from the Army, Navy, and
Commerce and Industry Ministries and the Board of Information.
CULTURAL AND PROPAGANDA FILMS
The authorities have decided that the distribution
of cultural films in Japan Proper will be carried out en-
tirely by the Japan News Eigashi (Japan News Cinema Co.)
according to recent reports in the local press. This
decision has apparently been made in order to ensure smooth
distribution and to take such films out of the realm of
business competition. The Japan News Eigashi has sent Mr. S.
Taguohi

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
Taguchi, the chief of its foreign section, to the United
States where he is expected to remain for about two years.
Mr. Taguchi's purpose in going to the United States is sup-
posed to be an effort to secure important and interesting
American newsreels for exhibition in Japan (Note 1).
It was announced early in March that the Japan
Culture Central Federation will hold an art festival in
commemoration of the 2,60lst anniversary of the founding of
the Japanese Empire in December 194l. A film competition
will be held on this occasion, with a first prize of Yen
3,000 and three other prizes of Yen 1,000 each. Any pro-
ducer may submit up to five culture films, which must be
35 millimeter sound production, approved by the Education
Ministry and completed or released between May and the end
of November of this year.
The Education Ministry has reportedly decided on
a five year plan for installing motion picture projectors
in all of the National Schools. Films will be used for
classes in history, geography and science. At the present
time, there are only about 5,000 projectors in possession
of the primary schools, which number about 20,000. Among
the total number, 4,500 are small size silent machines, 230
standard silent machines, 30 sound projectors and 240
unspecified. It is estimated that the National Schools,
which were established on April 1, 194l, will require about
40,000 projectors.
A number of cultural and propaganda pictures have
recently been sent to foreign countries. Although these
are

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
are not usually announced in the press, it is reported that
three Japanese war films will be sent to Germany in July.
German versions have been made in Tokyo by Dr. Erwin T.
Baelz. The pictures involved are "Skies in Flames",
"Captain Nishizumi, Commander of a Tank Unit" and "Five
Scout Soldiers". Another film, "Crystals of Snow", which
was shown at the International Snow Conference held in
Washington, will be sent to German y by reguest.
The Society of International Cultural Relations 1s
reportedly planning to send about a dozen Japanese cultural
films to Thailand, as a result of a request from that
country. The films involved are said to be "Warm Currents",
"Bugaku", "Japanese Woodblock Prints", "Bamboo Handicraft",
"Gems of the Sea", "Paradise of Skiers", "The Little Messenger"
and "Loochoo Dances". Other films are being shown in French
Indo-China by the French Indo-China Cinema Association.
Motion picture theatres showing exclusively Japanese films
are expected to be opened up shortly at Hanoi and Haiphong.
Further evidence of the penetration of Japanese
cultural films was given in a recent report in the Miyako
Shimbun. This report stated that Mr. Torrentino, an
important Italian motion picture distributor who is
supposed to have control over 5oo chain theatres in Brazil,
has approached the Japanese Foreign Office with regard to the
shipment of Japanese films to Brazil. Mr. Torrentino is
quoted as stating that he did not favor most American films
because "they are too dogmatic and lack universal appeal",
and that the supply from Europe is extremely limited. The
Sooiety

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
Society of International Cultural Relations is scheduled to send
a number of newsreels and culture films to Brazil, and, if
these are well received, other types of pictures will also
be sent regularly.
THE "NEW STRUCTURE" FILM POLICY
Plans for a drastic reorganization of the film
industry in order to induce better cooperation with the
"new structure" and the Asia "co-prosperity sphere" are
gradually beginning to take shape. The preliminary stages
have taken the form of criticism of the existing motion
picture distributing firms, on the grounds that they are
primarily interested in making a profit and not in advanc-
ing the culture and solidarity of the nation. Under the
present system, pictures pronounced "excellent" by the
Education Ministry are still shown only at the theatres
controlled by the producers, but it is contended that such
pictures should be distributed to all chains, regardless of
their connections.
Another step towards regimentation of the industry
is the plan to reorganize the Japan Motion Picture Association
so that it can serve as a liaison organization between the
Government and the producers and distributors. It has
recently been decided that the Association shall be enlarged
to inolude the Nihon Eiga Jigyo Renmeika1 (Japan Motion
Pioture Enterprise Association), the Nihon Eigajin Renme1
(Japan Motion Picture Personnel Association) and the Nihon
Eisha Gijutsuka Kyokai (Japan Motion Picture Photographera'
Association). These bodies will all be merged into one
general

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
一7-
general group, which will thus include not only theatre
owners, producers and office staffs, but also actors, mechanics,
photographers, etc.
Mergers of the small film studios are planned in
the near future under official instructions, according to
a news report in the Kokumin Shimbun. Although the larger
companies will maintain their separate entity, all of the
small firms must unite in order to facilitate the operation
of the raw film distribution system.
The application of the "new order" policies 1s
also manifest in proposals for the establishment of a fixed
maximum price for motion pictures. Price fixing in this
field has not been attempted heretofore, in view of the
wide variation in the cost of individual productions. Re-
cently, however, theatre owners, particularly those in
charge of second and third rate establishments, have been
complaining that rates are much too high. The authorities
are now studying information on hand regarding production
costs in an effort to arrive at some basis for price fixing.
It is reported that this policy will also be applied to
imported motion pictures, but since the Government has
no way of ascertaining the cost of production in foreign
countries, such a program will prove exceedingly difficult.
Some action restricting unusually high rates for imported
films will probably be put into effect, however, in the
course of time.
MOTION

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
一8-
MOTION PICTURE THEATRES AND ATTENDANCE
According to a recent press report presumably
based on official data, there were a total of 2,086 motion
picture houses in Japan Proper on January 1, 194l. This
figure, however, includes 198 theatres which do not show
films regularly. An analysis of the location of these mo-
tion picture houses shows that Tokyo leads with a total of
317, followed by Osaka with 191 and Fukuoka with 117.
Although the attendance at second and third rate
theatres has fallen off, the total number of tickets sold
in Tokyo during 1940 was reported to be 101,768,711, as com-
pared with 91,648,012 for the preceding year. This increase
has occurred in spite of the opposition of the authorities
to amusements of any sort, and may be said to reflect the
high pay received by workers in the munitions and other
wartime industries, which can no longer be spent on tangible
goods, owing to the present shortages of food, clothing, etc.
A near riot occurred at one large motion picture theatre
in Tokyo, owing to the crowds which could not obtain ad-
mission. Police intervention was necessary to restore
order. This affair provoked broadsides from the press and
official circles regarding the "insincere" attitude of the
people during the time of crisis, and caused stricter en-
foroement of the regulations regarding the selling of
tickets after the full accomodation capacity has been
reached.
CENSORSHIP

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
CENSORSHIP DATA FOR 1940
Motion pictures inspected by the Censorship Bureau
of the Home Ministry during 1940 totalled 112,25l reels
aggregating 25,901,139 meters, according to a vernacular
press report. Of this total, 13,903 reels consisted of
1mported films, while the remaining 98,348 reels represented
domestic production. Figures for 1939 were not available
but in 1938, 113,084 reels totalling 25,920,648 meters were
inspeoted. Both foreign and domestic films showed slight
declines as compared with the 1938 data.
ACTORS' SCHOOL ESTABLISHED
Press reports state that a school for the
training of motion picture actors will be opened in April.
This project is the result of dissatisfaction in some
quarters with the inferior acting ability of many of the
performers, which is attributed primarily to lack of train-
ing. The school will receive as pupils, boys who have
graduated from college or some corresponding institution
and girls who have completed Higher School education.
NEW INVENTIONS
A method of converting old silent movie projectors
into sound equipment has reportedly been invented by a
Tokyo mechanic, Mr. S. Yumoto. The main problem is alleged
to have centered about the replacement of the movable
"gate", for permitting the entry of light, with an immovable
one. It is expeoted that this new method will be applied
to many of the 8,Ooo obeolete projectors which are now
1d1e.

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
Mr. S. Kuwamura, a technician in the motion
picture department of the Miyako Company 1s said to have
perfeoted an apparatus designed to stop the motion of the
projector instantly in case the film breaks or some accident
occurs in the machine. This invention, which is supposed
to materially reduce the risk of fire in the projection
room, has been recommended by the Home, Education and War
Ministries.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
May 6, 1941.
TA/H
Mr. Hornbeck:
If the deposit arrangement with the
Mitsui Trust Company contains a stipulation
that in case the Ministry of Finance allows
the remittance of any part or the whole of the
deposited funds to the United States, they may
be withdrawn from time to time for that purpose,
it would not seem to be open to objection as
conflicting with a policy of discouraging
American loans to Japan. That clause would
mitigate the five-year term of the deposit, and
would leave the companies or this Government
free to endeavor to obtain the withdrawal of
the funds from Japan.
As long as the funds are blocked in Japan
in liquid form they do constitute an involuntary
extension of American credit to the Japanese
economy. That is the effect of blocking foreign
funds which would otherwise be withdrawn from
the country. Even if the funds could be with-
drawn from a bank and invested at will in direct
property owmership in Japan, the Japanese pro-
hibition of withdrawal from the country would
still have the effect of causing an involuntary
extension of American credit to the Japanese.
The Mitsui proposal for keeping the funds liquid
in case it becomes possible to withdraw them
from the country seems about as favorable a
solution as could be made consistently with
earning any current return on the funds.

MOTION

was communicated to this Embassy e report received
by the Japanese authorities concerning a motion pic-
PICTURES/62
ture entitled "The Uniform" being produced by Metro-
Goldwyn-Mayer Company. This picture is distasteful
to the Japanese authorities because, if the report is
acourate, in one scene it portrays Japanese soldiers
in an extremely compromising situation. While I am
not aware that the Japanese authorities have undertaken
to disprove or even to deny all the numerous reports
circulated abroad of excesses committed by Japanese
soldiers in the course of the campaign in China, never-
theless, in Japan a soldier is respected as the repre-
Mr. Will H. Hays, President,

28 West 44th Street,

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
sentative of the Emperor and reflections upon his honor

Therefore, without raising the question of what-
ever faotual basis there may be for the portrayal of
Japanese soldiers in such an unfavorable light, the
present complaint has been predicated upon the unfor-
tunate effect upon relations between the United States
and Japan which may result from further exacerbation
of public opinion due to unfavorable motion picture
publicity.
So far as I am aware, the unsuccessful representa-
tions addressed by the Japanese Embassy to our Department
of State, to which the Japanese official made reference,
concerned only matters appearing in American publications
and not in American motion picture productions. The
Japanese authorities may well be justified in assuming,
however, that the American Government would be obliged
to rejeot on the same grounds (freedom of the American
press) representations which the Japanese authorities
might make in regard to the present matter.
You will appreciate that in my position I cannot
be indifferent to events affeoting the state of publio
opinion in the United States and Japan and that I should
like to be able to eliminate, whenever possible, un-
necessary causes of friction in the relations of the

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
two countries. It is this concern which has prompted
me to address you thus informally and without consult-
ing the State Department in regard to a matter which
cannot fail to be most distressing to Japanese.
It appears that the Japanese authorities are con-
vinoed that any formal protest in regard to a motion
picture production would not only be unproductive of
results but might even serve to heighten anti-Japanese
feeling in the United States, and that therefore they
have chosen this more or less personal manner of appeal
as offering the only hope of finding a means of amelior-

than thus informally to present the facts for your con-
sideration. Of course I would not presume to advise
you concerning what action, if any, is feasible under
the ciroumstances, and in submitting the foregoing I
am motivated only by a desire to draw attention to con-
siderations of an international character whioh may not
have been fully appreciated or accurately gauged when
production of "The Uniform" was undertaken. I have not
myself seen the film under reference.
Sincerely yours,
Joseph C. Grew

: Informal Complaint against Anti-Japanese

informed by Mr. Yuki that his purpose in wishing to
see me was to communicate to the Embassy the substance
of a report which had been received at the Foreign
Office concerning an objectionable picture which is
being filmed by Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer. He stated that
the title of the pioture is "The Uniform" and that
the scene is laid in interior China and Burma. The
part of the film which the Japanese authorities find
objeotionable is a scene in which Japanese soldiers
are represented as being on the point of "violating"
Chinese girls. At the crucial moment, Clark Gable

designs of the Japanese soldiers (Mr. Yuki remarked

soldiers).

authorities who see in a picture of this sort a means
of stirring up ill-feeling between thepeoples of Japan

magazines as LIFE and TIME might bring it to the attention

Sir:
Following my meeting with Mr. Adams, in Washington, on
May 21, 194l, in reference to the purchase by Paramount Films, Ltd.
MOTi ON PiCTURES/67
in Japan of Jepanese Government bonds and the commi tment of such
Company made to the Japanese Government to purchase additional bonds,
it seemed to me desirable that I set forth briefly the facts involved
in this transaction. Paramount Films, Ltd., on whose behalf this
letter is written, is a corporation organized under the laws of the
State of New Jersey, all of the outstanding capital stock of which
is owmed by Paramount Pictures Inc., a New York corporation, and the
writer is the General Manager of the interests of Paramount Pictures
Inc. in the Far East and is personally familiar with the subject
matter of this letter.
Briefly, the facts in reference to this matter are as folloms:
(A) Paramount Films, Ltd., which distributes Paramount
motion pioture productions in Japan, entered into
PS/
negotiations with the Japanese Government in late 
November, l940, involving the acquisition by it of bnd
out of funds arising from its distribution over a 
AG
ED
period of more than two years of such productions
in Japan and which it was unable, due to regulationsto
414
of the Japanese Government, to remit to Paramount
Piotures Inc.; further The National City Bank of
New York, Tokyo, in December, l940, advised
Paramount Films, Ltd. to the effect that funts

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
AMOUNT PICTURES INC.
May 23, 1941
Henerable Cordell Hull
-2-
deposited by the Company with the Bank were at the
risk of the Company.
(B) Since such proposed investment of funds of Paramount
Films, Ltd. was not in the usual course of business,
the matter was referred to Paramount Pictures Inc.,
in New York, and by a cable, dated January 3, 1941,
the undersigned was advised by the Manager of the
Foreign Department of Paramount Pictures Inc. to the
effect that the proposed investment in an amount up
to eighty per cent (80%) of the acoumulated and
accumulating cash of Paramount Films, Ltd. would be
approved upon condition that permission was obtained
to remit in United States currency the interest payable
on the bonds and an agreement was obtained from the
government to repurchase the bonds at the priee paid
therefor. Such Foreign Manager while not making it a
condition of the purchase of the bonds urged that
efforts be made to obtain in connection with the pur-
chase permission to make an additional remittance in
United States currency.
(C) The negotiations with the Japenese Government continued
until some time prior to January 31, l94l, on or before
which date a definite commitment was made by Paramount
Films, Ltd. to purchase at par a total of yen 1,400,000
principal amount of Japanese Government bonds, interest
thereon at the rate of three and one-half per cent (3t%)
per annum to be remittable to New York at the official
rate in United States currency, the aggregate amount of
yen 750,000 to be purchased as soon as the checks in
payment of the purchase price could be signed by the writer,
whoms, at that time, absent from Japan and the balance
of such principal amount, namely, yen 650,000 to be purchased
from time to time as funds became available to Paramount
Films, Ltd. The terms of purchase also included the com-
mitment by the Japanese Government to permit Paramount
Films, Ltd. to remit to New York at the official rate,
in United States currency, an amount equal to ten per cent
(lo%) of the purchase price of the bonds as and when the
same.were purchased, which commitment obviously made the
transaotion particularly favorable to Paramount Films, Ltd.
(D) About the middle of February, l94l, the representative
in Japan of Paramount Films, Ltd., Mr. John Albeck,
first learned that the Department of State would not look
with favor on the purchase by Paramount Films, Ltd. of
such bonds, Mr. Albeck not having been informed of the

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 5824 of September 2, 1941,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Tokyo, August 28, 1941.
Memorandum to the Ambassador:
This afternoon I was visited by a delegation com-
prising representatives of all the American motion picture
companies now operating in Japan. These men informed me
that each one of them recently has received demands from
their staffs for a written guarantee of retirement allowances
to be paid in the event these companies found it necessary
to close their businesses in Japan as a result of present
adverse business conditions and the possible extension
of the recently enforced monopoly on Japanese pictures to
Americen pictures.
The various staffs are demanding retirement allowances
of two years' salary plus three months' salary for each
year of service. This would mean that an employee who
had been working for a company for ten years would receive
the equivalent of 4 1/2 years' salary as a retirement
allowance. Furthermore, the total amount involved for the
eight companies on this basis would aggregate well over
one million yen.
The companies have decided to handle this matter
individually and have notified their staffs that they
would agree only to an allowance of one month's salary for
each year of service. This offer has been rejected, and
during the last two days indignation meetings have been
held in the offices of several of the companies wi thout
permission of the manager. Furthermore, the managers have
been

=== Page 261 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
been threatened with police detention and questioning, and
also with not being allowed to leave Japan in the event
they are ordered by their home offices to close their
businesses.
Yesterday the Japanese Government decided to enforce
a monopoly system on Japanese pictures confining all
motion picture production to one central company and all
distribution to another central company. It is anticipated
that the next move will be to establish a similar monopoly
over American pictures. If such a step is taken it is the
opinion of the representatives of the American motion
picture companies that their home offices will not agree
to operate under such conditions.
In my opinion this is just another instance of
forcing American companies out of Japan. At present these
eight American motion picture concerns have over eleven and
a half million yen frozen in Japan, and some of the
managers of these ∞ ncerns have been frankly told that
the police authorities at least are of the opinion that the
Japanese staffs should share liberally in these accumulated
profits.
The position of these firms is becoming extremely
critical as the Japanese employees seem determined to go
to any extremes to force compliance with their demands.
We have had reports of almost similar demands being made
on other American firms in Japan. I am, therefore, very
strongly of the opinion that this situation should be
brought to the attention of the Japanese Government and
that representation should be made urging adequate pro-
teotion to American firms against suoh exhorbitant demands
from their Japanese staffs.
FRANK S. WILLIAMS
Commercial Attaché

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 5824 dated September 2, 1941
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Copy of an Oral Statement left by the Ambassador, Mr.
Grew, with the Chief of the American Bureau of the
Foreign Office, Mr. Terasaki, on August 29, 194l.
Oral
The American Embassy is seriously disturbed over
reports reaching it concerning the exorbitant demands
being made by their Japanese staffs upon American firms
in Japan which, for one reason or another, may be forced
to close out their business.
Representatives of the American motion picture com-
panies now operating in Japan have informed the Embassy
that each one of them has recently received demands from
their staffs for a written guarantee of retirement
allowances to be paid in the event that these companies
found it necessary to close their business in Japan as
a result of present adverse business conditions and the
possible extension of the recently enforced monopoly on
Japanese pictures to American pictures. The staffs of
these companies are demanding retirement allowances of
two years' salary plus three months' salary for each
year of service. This would mean that an employee who
had been working for a company for ten years would re-
ceive the equivalent of four and one-half years' salary
as a retirement allowance; furthermore, the total amount
involved for the eight companies on this basis would
total well over one million yen.
The representatives of the companies state that
they have notified their staffs that they would agree
only to the customary allowance of one month's salary
for each year of service but that this offer has been
rejeoted

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
rejected and that as a result indignation meetings have
been held in the offices of several of the companies
without permission of the manager, and the managers have
been threatened with police detention and questioning and
with possible refusal of permission to leave the country
when the time came for them to do so.
It is to be hoped that the Japanese Government will
take cognizance of the difficulties, such as those
described above, suffered by American firms, and that
steps may be taken to extend protection to all American
firms against such threats and exorbitant demands from
their Japanese staffs.
August 29, 1941.

My dear Mr. Dunne:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of
August 13 concerning the 2-reel Japanese motion picture
entitled Our Fleet which you have been informed is in
the custody of the Department.
894.4061 MOTION PICTURES
I have checked with the interested divisions of
the Department and none of the officers with whom I
spoke seem to know anything about such a film. It is
possible that the Navy Department has it and that they
would be willing to make it available for your use in
producing a picture on the war in the Pacific. I should
iike very mueh to see your film when it is completed.
I shall try to see you on my next trip to New York,
and in the meantime if you get down to "ashington be
894.4061mol
sure to drop in at my office.
Sincerely yours,
John M. Begg
Assistant Chief
Division of Cultural Relations
